How might an internship evolve into a career in data science?
2026-09-03

How might an internship evolve into a career in data science?

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
Accenture’s Andrew Kelly explores the early days of his career amid Ireland’s data science landscape and how others might forge a similar career path. Andrew Kelly, a senior manager in AI and data at Accenture, was undertaking a degree in engineering when he first started out as an intern at the organisation. He explained that it was the first time that he had real exposure to data science, through working with data to find patterns and produce insights. He told SiliconRepublic.com, “I had always enjoyed maths and quantitative subjects, but this was different. It was hands-on, applied and had a clear commercial reason behind it. Something clicked. I went back to college, completed my master’s in engineering, but already knew where I wanted to end up.” Soon after, Kelly re-joined Accenture as a graduate and immediately began to work once again within the realm of analytics, but also started to branch out into business intelligence reporting, data engineering, GenAI, financial services and, increasingly, banking. Kelly said, “In recent years my focus has shifted from being a data practitioner to helping banking clients build the foundations for AI at scale – the platforms, the governance, the risk frameworks and the responsible AI processes that have to sit behind all of it – while simultaneously trying to deliver real, measurable value quickly enough to prove it works.” As Kelly finds it, Ireland is “in a promising but unfinished position” within a space that has allowed for the sharp acceleration of AI adoption. According to a previous report published by Accenture, the number of Irish employees using GenAI tools daily is almost triple the figure noted just two years ago. However, he is of the opinion that this has yet to translate into a more visible and effective organisational transformation, noting it is really only taking effect at an individual level, rather than across whole companies. “What I see consistently in organisations is the treatment of AI as a technology initiative rather than a business reinvention. There is a strong temptation to automate isolated steps in a process, to take a multi-step workflow and automate part of it,” said Kelly. “That is not transformation. The organisations that will pull ahead are those asking a fundamentally different question: ‘How does AI change how we work end-to-end?’ or ‘How does it change how we interact with customers?’ That requires process reinvention, not point automation.” There is a gap shown in the research, he finds, with only around 10pc of Irish organisations having reached what Accenture would classify as ‘scaler’ status – that is, where AI is embedded in core operations. The majority are still experimenting and integrating, largely in silos. “The data, governance and legacy infrastructure challenges are real constraints. But the deeper issue is often strategic clarity. AI investment needs to be value-led, tied to top-level business outcomes, not driven by the technology itself. “The encouraging signal is that Ireland has the right ingredients – talent, infrastructure, regulatory framework and genuine national ambition. The question is execution at scale, and that clock is ticking.” Though it wasn’t all that long ago, much has changed in the data science and wider STEM space since Kelly first began his career. He explained that at the start of his journey, work was often centred on the development of predictive models in code and running data science projects that served specific parts of the business. Conversations were “largely technical” and outcomes were “meaningful but relatively contained in their organisational reach”. “What has changed fundamentally is the scope of impact. GenAI still requires deep expertise to build and govern well, but its reach extends far beyond those who build it. It is permeating processes across entire organisations, touching roles, workflows and customer interactions that traditional machine learning never reached in the same way.” Skill expectations have somewhat transformed too. Nowadays, workforce-wide AI readiness has emerged as a critical capability. “AI literacy can no longer sit only with the teams building the technology. It must be distributed across the business. The thing that has not changed is the pace of change itself. It has always been fast in technology, but it is accelerating in ways that organisations must actively manage,” said Kelly. “The technical foundation matters – understanding data engineering, machine learning, platform architecture and governance well enough to have credible conversations with both practitioners and senior clients. But in my day-to-day, the skills I lean on most are not technical ones.” Instead, he regularly utilises a broad skillset that enables him to navigate complex environments with professionals in regulation, risk, tech, business and senior leadership, “often simultaneously and with competing priorities”. He added, “The ability to translate between technical teams and business decision-makers, between what AI can do and what it should do, is probably the skill I use most.” But most of all, the attribute that often stands out the most in a career setting is not a perfect track record, but a display of genuine interest. Kelly himself came into his initial role as an intern, moving from engineering into data science, and he often wondered in the early days if his technical profile compared sufficiently with those of people who had studied computer science or statistics directly. “That was misplaced. What I had was curiosity and a willingness to learn and, looking back, that counted for far more. The second thing, and this is something I still have to remind myself of, is to make peace with not knowing everything. In AI especially, that feeling of being slightly behind the curve is essentially universal. “Everyone is learning with this technology. The organisations and individuals who are pulling ahead are not necessarily the ones with the most prior knowledge. They are the ones with the highest aptitude and appetite for learning continuously as things change. “What I look for when I hire is exactly that – the ability to pick things up, adapt and grow. Skills can be acquired. That orientation, that genuine curiosity and resilience is harder to teach. If you have it, the rest tends to follow.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. United Nations Concedes That Efforts to Arrest Climate Change Have Failed, and the Planet Is Now Spiraling Into Environmental Catastrophe

United Nations Concedes That Efforts to Arrest Climate Change Have Failed, and the Planet Is Now Spiraling Into Environmental Catastrophe

Futurism | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. With so many climate projections and mitigation strategies eyeing far-away years like 2050, it can be tempting to assume there’s still time to reverse the worst impacts of an ever-warming planet. Unfortunately, that’s no longer in the cards — at least according to the United Nations Environment Programme, which just released a dismal report calling for the “world to face the inevitability of exceeding global warming of 1.5 degrees Celsius” above pre-industrial levels. That’s significant, because the 1.5 degree threshold has long been associated with a range of grim climate tipping points at which the planet will experience irreversible consequences such as the “breakdowns of major ocean circulation systems, abrupt thawing of boreal permafrost, and collapse of tropical coral reef systems,” per a UN climate explainer. The new UN paper, coming on the heels of the hottest summer on record and pessimistically titled “Limiting Overshoot,” identifies the consequences of this reality, and recommends some immediate actions to keep things from spiraling even further. Though most scientists agree we’re hovering around the 1.4 degree point at the moment, the UN notes that even in the best case scenario, where “all climate commitments and policies are implemented, warming is projected to reach 1.8 degrees Celsius at a minimum.” If we follow our current track, however, the report highlights the distinct possibility of hitting 2.6 degrees by the year 2100. That catastrophic future is far from certain, although drastic intervention is becoming increasingly urgent if we hope to stave it off. Underscoring the severity of the present moment, the UN frames the situation in terms of maintaining warming at the 1.5 degree mark — no longer a theoretical ceiling, but the new floor going forward. Doing so, the report authors write, depends “on achieving sustained net-negative CO2 emissions at global scale in the second half of this century, and potentially further reductions of non-CO2 emissions.” “Decisions taken this decade will determine whether viable options remain to gradually reverse warming back to 1.5 degrees Celsius,” they explain. Speaking to CBC News, UN Environment Programme executive director Inger Andersen said “let’s be very clear, 1.5 target is still the goal.” “But now we need to approach it differently from above,” Andersen explained. “Every tenth of a degree we avoid still means less damage.” More on climate change: It’s Official: This Is the Hottest America Has Ever Been

China Installing Huge Facility That Turns Plastic Waste Into Jet Fuel
2026-09-03

China Installing Huge Facility That Turns Plastic Waste Into Jet Fuel

Futurism | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. Around the world, the demand for cleaner alternatives to petroleum-based jet fuel is rising. By 2030, the European Union will require a minimum sustainable aviation fuel (SAF) blend of 6 percent, which will rise to 70 percent by 2050. Countries like the United Kingdom, Singapore, Japan, and Brazil are instituting similar policies, putting pressure on players in the global energy trade to ease reliance on crude oil. SAF can come from all kinds of recycled waste byproducts like plant material, animal fat, and used cooking oil — the latter of which has already become a major source for existing SAF supplies. Unfortunately, the flow of extra spent fry oil is already slowing to a trickle as refiners swoop in to secure the gloop. That’s where Chinese company Niutech comes in. In a new press release, first spotted by Interesting Engineering, Niutech announced a massive contract with an anonymous “global energy major” to build out an “industrial continuous waste plastic pyrolysis production line” capable of churning out 10,000 to 50,000 tons of plastic-derived SAF each year. That might seem like a lot to unpack, but it’s really pretty simple. Basically, the Chinese firm is announcing the development of a massive supply line that turns plastic garbage into usable jet fuel. It plans to do so using a thermal decomposition process called pyrolysis, in which plastic waste is heated to extraordinary temperatures in an oxygen-free environment. Though it isn’t new, pyrolysis remains something of a niche for the industrial-grade plastics-recycling space. For this approach to meet the growing need for SAF, it first has to graduate beyond the realm of small eco-startups into a widely-accepted industry standard — which appears to be Niutech’s goal. Unlike previous SAF initiatives involving plastics, this particular production line will all be under one roof, cutting down on the need for costly shipping and handling. The Niutech refinery is also said to make use of a smorgasbord of plastics like polyethylene, nylon, polypropylene, and ABS, all “without complex pre-treatment such as washing, sorting or drying” — streamlining the complex process of sorting plastic and converting it to refinable oil at this kind of scale. Should Niutech and its anonymous industry partner succeed in bringing the system online, it would undoubtedly serve as a constant source of plastics-based SAF for the global aviation industry, and as a role model for other refineries to follow. “Large-scale pyrolysis projects impose stringent requirements on continuous operational capability,” a Niutech spokesperson said in the company’s press release. “Niutech has focused on pyrolysis technology for nearly 40 years and brings mature equipment and extensive project experience. This partnership is another validation of the strength of our industrial continuous technology.” More on China: American Tech Companies Are Suddenly Sweating Bullets as China Catches Up on AI

Toronto Blue Jays Roasted for Posting AI Slop Promoting Nestle, Claiming It’s the Work of a Human Animator

Futurism | 2026-09-03

The animation industry is facing an existential threat in the form of generative AI. Entertainment execs remain adamant that the tech is the answer, allowing fewer workers to accomplish more in less time — a thinly-veiled attempt, critics charge, to pinch pennies and churn out lower quality content. Marketing teams are also frequently caving to the irresistible draw of text-to-video generators, a cutting of corners in ways that are both uninspired and easy to spot. And as the backlash to generative AI continues to grow, especially in creative fields, those attempts are bringing in far more negative than positive attention. Case in point, fans of the Major League Baseball team the Toronto Blue Jays were outraged after the team’s social media account posted an animated short, as part of a brand tie-in with Nestle-owned beverage company Nestea. The clip, animated in a style typical for a TV show aimed at adults, like “Bojack Horseman” or “Family Guy,” bears all the hallmarks of the sloppy use of AI, from garbled, misoriented hands to illegible text. A community note appended to the post points out that the video “contains AI artifacts including garbled text on trading card boxes and a player with three arms in the rookie poster, contrary to the claim it was made by a real animator.” Instead of owning up to the lazy use of AI, the team dug in its heels. “Corny,” one fan wrote. “[Blue Jays owner] Rogers can’t afford real animation??” “Corny comment,” the team’s official account replied. “This was made by a real animator.” The doubling down in particular didn’t sit well with fans. “It’s not just AI, It’s really painfully obvious error riddled AI,” one user tweeted. “Yeahhh, you ain’t fooling anybody,” another wrote. “Do better.” “Plenty of talented animators you could have hired to do this instead of having it be AI slop,” one user commented on an extended version posted to YouTube. “I fear you got fleeced paying whoever made this, it’s very obvious [AI] was used especially with the background details,” another added. The incident perfectly illustrates widespread disillusionment with the use of generative AI and how radioactive the topic has become. We’ve gotten to the point where small businesses are starting to hand draw their own promotional signage and vow not to use any AI, as 404 Media reported last month — getting a major influx of interest as a result. It’s a particularly precarious situation for human animators, who’ve watched their industry change dramatically over the years. Even the creator of the iconic stop motion franchise “Wallace and Gromit” recently said he’d start to “embrace” AI. Leaning into the tech can easily backfire. Emmy-winning writer director Jorge Gutierrez announced he was partnering up with Amazon as part of its GenAI Creators’ Fund earlier this year, but the backlash turned out to be fierce enough for him to walk away from the deal altogether. Zohran Mamdani Bans AI for NYC Public School Students Up to Eighth Grade
2026-09-03

Zohran Mamdani Bans AI for NYC Public School Students Up to Eighth Grade

Futurism | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. New York City just instituted a sweeping ban on schools using AI in the classroom through the start of high school, along with other restrictions on the technology. Mayor Zohran Mamdani announced the one-year moratorium on Wednesday, calling it the “most comprehensive of its kind anywhere in the country.” “The tech industry wants us to believe that AI in early education is not only inevitable, but that it is necessary,” Mamdani said during a press conference. “We do not see it that way. I have yet to see a study showing that AI is beneficial for students in elementary and middle school, with the exception, of course, of research sponsored by the very corporations that stand to profit.” Under the new AI policy, the school system will “fully discontinue or disable more” than 38 AI features that were part of previously allowed programs that don’t meet the new standards, Mamdani said. Ailish Brady, the deputy mayor’s senior education adviser, said that includes an AI reading assistant called Amira, Politico noted. Young students, starting with kids in 2-K (the city’s free education and daycare program for two-year-olds), won’t use AI as part of class until they enter high school. Even then, its use will be closely controlled. High schools will take limited “AI literacy” classes to prepare students for what Mamdani called an “AI-saturated world.” Exceptions will be made for helping students with learning disabilities or are still learning English. The restrictions also don’t apply to certain approved instructional programs like e-books and coding software. AI isn’t the only tech the policy grapples with. It also imposes strict limits on screen time, recommending no more than 30 minutes of using devices per day for students in third through fifth grade, and 45 minutes for middle schoolers. Beyond the broader debate raging on AI’s role in education — with colleges across the country embroiled in AI cheating scandals, as well as a bevy of research suggesting AI impairs critical thinking and other cognitive abilities —  the ban comes after months of New York City facing heated political debate on using the tech in the classroom. In April, controversy erupted over plans to open an AI-focused high school in Manhattan, with a crowd of parents, teachers, and high schoolers protesting outside City Hall. While the plans were withdrawn soon afterwards, it amplified calls on Mamdani to institute an AI moratorium. While deeming the moratorium a meaningful step in the right direction, some hoped Mamdani would’ve gone further. Kelly Clancy, the founder of Parents for AI Caution and a member of the Coalition for an AI Moratorium, wanted a temporary moratorium on AI in all grades. Some of the original calls for a moratorium demanded that it last at least two years. “The fact that this policy exists at all is due to the enormous work of parents across the city who spoke out against the overreach of AI into their kids’ classroom,” Clancy told the New York Times. The aggressive action will be closely watched by other states and school systems. New York City boasts the largest school system in the nation, with the ban affecting more than 600,000 students. It will be a large, and indeed, “comprehensive” experiment in how detoxing young minds on AI will affect their learning — though it pales in size to the experiment that tech companies are conducting by unleashing the tech on students in the first place. “Children need their teachers and human connection in order to learn and in order to grow,” Mamdani said. “And they need to develop skills alongside their peers, build relationships with their teachers, and wrestle with tough problems on their own.” More on education: Scientists Gave 12-Year-Olds Access to an AI Tutor and You Probably Already Know What They Did With It

World Plunged Into Chaos as ChatGPT, Claude, and Grok Suddenly Go Down Simultaneously: “Finally I Can See the Sun!”

As if a million white collar workers suddenly cried out in terror at once, a great disturbance in the AI force forced down every major AI chatbot service — a brief moment of reprieve from our AI empire overlords. OpenAI’s ChatGPT, Anthropic’s Claude, Google’s Gemini, and X’s Grok mysteriously went down Thursday morning, and we still don’t know what’s behind it. “All the AI is down,” as one X user put it buntly. OpenAI’s status page noted “elevated errors across ChatGPT and Codex.” “We have applied the mitigation and are monitoring the recovery,” the company wrote. While X’s Grok shows widespread outages, Anthropic’s Claude status page noted that the “only affected models right now are Opus 4.8 and Opus 5.” “The rest of the models have recovered to baseline error rate,” Anthropic’s website reads. Spiking and falling reported incidents on Downdetector suggest outages affecting OpenAI’s services have largely been resolved. Other services, including Google and the popular video game “Fortnite,” continue to experience issues. Netizens were quick to joke about out the unusual outage affecting almost every major AI chatbot service, describing a momentary glimpse of lucidity for a society obsessed with a tech that’s deteriorating our cognitive abilities. “Finally I can see the Sun!” one account tweeted. “And for a brief moment, millions of people had to use their brains again,” journalist and noted AI critic Paris Marx quipped in a Bluesky post. “If you are renting your brain, what happens during global rent-a-brain outage?” another user joked. TechCrunch
The sameness problem behind those unappetizing AI-generated menus
2026-09-04

The sameness problem behind those unappetizing AI-generated menus

TechCrunch | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
When it first happens to you, you think you’re crazy. You wander into a cafe and look at a menu with a variety of bagel sandwiches, but each illustration looks eerily flawless, precisely symmetrical, and oddly smooth, eliciting a visceral sensation that something isn’t right. You might think you’re paranoid, but you’re not losing your mind. Generative AI menus have hit the restaurant business courtesy of models trained on a narrow, “pleasing” aesthetic that feels wrong even when you can’t articulate why. Sometimes, these illustrations are egregiously fake, like a burrito with cheese so bubbly and melty that it looks more like avant garde art than lunch. More often, they’re so ordinary looking that you only notice something is wrong when you take a second to look more closely. “It’s almost like an alien trying to make a pizza without understanding its core principles,” Reality Defender CTO Alex Lisle told TechCrunch. (Reality Defender itself is part of a growing category of startups selling AI-detection and content-verification tools — a business that exists in part because of issues like this one.) Throw a rock in NYC and you’ll hit a mildly off putting AI food advertisement. pic.twitter.com/lLXVkFIFoG Lisle says that the way these models are built can help explain why illustrations seem to embrace such a specific aesthetic — one where every ice cream scoop is perfectly round, and where shrimp seem to have been genetically modified to eat their own tails, creating new “Lovecraftian food horrors.” Large language models (LLMs) and diffusion models — the kinds of AI models that make seemingly omniscient chatbots and image generators like ChatGPT and Midjourney possible — are trained on vast quantities of data. The models then identify patterns in the datasets to predict what a user is looking for when they ask something like, “Make me a menu for a burger restaurant.” “A lot of this stuff looks like a Chili’s menu from 2015, and there’s a reason for that,” Lisle said. “That was the corpus of work from which [the models] drew their function.” New training data is invaluable to the companies building AI models — Amazon has even been found to source rare books to scan and add to its training data, only to destroy those books once they’ve been uploaded. It’s inevitable that some AI-generated content will seep into these incomprehensibly large data sets. But when AI models train on too much of their own AI-generated content, they risk model collapse. “Model collapse is almost like a mad cow disease… when you feed the outputs from one model back into itself, eventually the inbreeding becomes too much, and the whole thing collapses,” Lisle explained. “What we see here is convergence, which isn’t necessarily model collapse.” Convergence is a bit less extreme, degrading the quality of an AI’s outputs without making it entirely useless. If someone asks an AI model to generate a menu for a fast food restaurant, the model will likely reference menus from Wendy’s, Burger King, McDonald’s, or another popular chain. These menus already share a similar style, which means that the AI-generated outputs will mimic that same style, only to further reinforce it further if the AI-generated menu ends up back in training data. But menus and advertisements for food will always look better than the real thing, like a Big Mac in a McDonald’s commercial where each layer of the sandwich is arranged by a prop designer to look maximally appetizing. This effect can become even more pronounced in AI outputs. “The optimization of the data sets is for pleasingness, or you know, not being offensive, and so there’s a way that turns into homogenization,” Lee Rainie, Director of the Imagining the Digital Future Center at Elon University, told TechCrunch. “What AI is known to do both in images and language is to shave off the edges.” On a more localized scale, this smoothing of images seems to happen when you use an AI image generator to create a menu and apply edits to it. On X, a user named Labtec showed what happens when you make a menu in ChatGPT, then edit it 100 times to see how the food continues to look less and less like it should. (We replicated the experiment and found similar results.) “The end result actually makes me uncomfortable,” Labtec wrote. I made a restaurant menu in ChatGPT then edited it 100 times to see how those hideous slop menus end up the way they are. The end result actually makes me uncomfortable pic.twitter.com/sDIUKlabdp Restaurants are likely falling victim to this problem, revising their AI-generated menus to alter small details over and over, like prices or item names. It seems that with each edit, the food images become a tiny bit more round and smooth. “People have an almost unexplainable sense about when they’re looking at something that’s AI-generated, compared with something that was real in the first place,” Rainie said. “There’s just a sensibility that people sometimes find hard to articulate, but they kind of know it when they see it and I think that’s one of the reasons why some of the early stories about the backlash [against restaurants using AI menus] is so pronounced.” There’s science behind our aversion to these AI menus. Researchers at the University of Duisburg-Essen in Germany found that AI-generated food images exhibited an “uncanny valley” effect, where images of food that looked almost real elicited more disgust and unease than images that were obviously fake. That squeamishness only intensifies in light of the cultural context around AI. If people react to these images so negatively, then that’s probably reason enough for restaurants to stop trying to make AI menus work. But the issues that bring us perfectly browned hamburger buns extend beyond the dinner table. “Seeing and hearing has always been believing, to the point where even our court systems are entirely tuned to the idea that the gold standard in evidence is taped confessions and videotaped evidence,” Lisle said. “That’s no longer the case. The world has fundamentally shifted, for good or for ill.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. This doesn’t affect our editorial independence. Data center developer Crusoe, which counts Meta, Microsoft, and OpenAI as its customers, has raised a new $3 billion round at a $30 billion valuation, Bloomberg reported. Oura files to go public
2026-09-03

Oura files to go public

TechCrunch | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
Oura, the smart ring maker, has filed to go public. The filing with the Securities and Exchange Commission on Thursday shows that Oura’s revenue has jumped substantially in the past year. The company went from $697 million in revenue during the nine-month period that ended June 30 last year to $1.2 billion during the corresponding period this year. The company has said previously that it generated $500 million in revenue in 2024, roughly $1 billion in 2025, and that it expected to generate close to $2 billion in revenue this year. The company says it has sold 3.6 million rings over the past year, and that it currently has approximately 5 million paid members — that is, users who subscribe to its service for broader health metrics. The filing also says that Oura has an approximately 85% weighted-average 12-month membership retention rate, meaning that roughly 85% of members who sign up in a given month are still subscribed a year later. The company’s rings, which sell in the range of $350 to $400, are, at their most fundamental level, fitness trackers designed to measure a user’s biometrics — everything from metabolism and heart rate to stress levels and sleep patterns. Paired with an app, the ring and that software are marketed by Oura as an “always-on health intelligence platform.” Late last month, it was reported that Oura was looking to raise $3 billion during its expected public offering. The company, which was founded in Finland in 2013, confidentially filed for an IPO in May. Bloomberg previously reported that the company is expected to seek a $16 billion valuation. In October of last year, it had been valued at around $11 billion. In its SEC filing, Oura outlines where it believes its audience can widen in the coming years. “We believe our opportunity extends beyond traditional wearable use cases centered on activity and fitness tracking,” it says. “We believe our platform can support significantly larger populations as we continue to expand access, build clinical evidence, and deepen integrations with health plans, employers, and care providers.” Oura also notes how its trove of data is fueling new AI integrations. “We believe we have amassed one of the largest and highest-quality longitudinal biometric datasets in consumer health, tracking over 50 health and wellness metrics and representing nearly 42 billion hours of physiological data,” it states. “This dataset powers our AI and machine-learning models, which decode complex physiological patterns and improve in accuracy, personalization, and predictive capability as member histories deepen.” TechCrunch has reached out to Oura for more information. The company, which now has offices throughout the world, including San Francisco, was recently hit with a proposed class action lawsuit accusing it of misleading users about the accuracy of its sleep tracking capabilities. The suit alleges Oura’s rings can’t actually detect the physiological signals needed to determine sleep stages, and instead rely on AI-generated estimates that the complaint describes as little more reliable than a coin flip. The litigation follows years of online complaints from users who said that Oura consistently rated their sleep as optimal when, in fact, it was not. The company has disputed the allegations and previously told TechCrunch it will “defend against” the claims in the “appropriate legal forum.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Tesla is asking people if they want to buy and run Cybercab fleets
2026-09-03

Tesla is asking people if they want to buy and run Cybercab fleets

TechCrunch | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
Tesla published a form on Thursday for businesses interested in buying Cybercab fleets or providing infrastructure for its network, the latest sign that the company’s aspirations for its gold-hued autonomous vehicle stretch beyond being a robotaxi operator. The robotaxi interest form, which was released ahead of the company’s Cybercab event in Austin, is not definitive proof that Tesla will sell its autonomous vehicles to third-party operators. But it’s certainly an indicator of where the company’s longer-term plans lie. Tesla wants to scale and it doesn’t seem to want to do it alone. Tesla CEO Elon Musk has talked often, and for years, about building a massive fleet of low-cost robotaxis. But in the early days, those dreams centered on personally owned Tesla vehicles. As early as 2016, Musk spoke publicly about a future in which Tesla owners, equipped with self-driving software, would be able to earn money by renting out their vehicles. He stuck with that Tesla network idea for years, noting at the company’s Autonomy Day in 2019 that it would allow owners to add their autonomous vehicles to its ride-sharing app, similar to how Uber’s business model works. “I feel very confident predicting that there will be autonomous robotaxis from Tesla next year — not in all jurisdictions because we won’t have regulatory approval everywhere,” Musk said in 2020. That vision never materialized. Instead, the company has focused on testing, and now operating, its own fleet of robotaxis — first with Tesla Model Y vehicles and now the purpose-built Cybercab. Until now, Tesla seemed committed to keeping its robotaxi business in-house. The interest form, which says “helps us build our robotaxi network,” suggests the company sees promise and profits in widening the circle to include third-party companies. What that might look like, though, isn’t defined. The company asks interested parties to pick one of several possible options, including Cybercab fleet purchasing, mobility hubs and infrastructure, event collaboration, and “other.” There are a growing number of companies jumping into the robotaxi fleet management business. For instance, Moove, an African fintech startup that initially focused vehicle financing for ride-hailing drivers, is scaling up an autonomous fleet management business. The startup, which raised $250 million last month at a $2.1 billion valuation, is the fleet operator for Waymo in Phoenix, Miami, and Las Vegas, and in the future, London. The company doesn’t own the Waymo vehicles, but its CEO told TechCrunch that it plans to. Other autonomous fleet management companies, which Uber has partnered with in its bid to own a piece of the robotaxi market, include Avomo and New Horizon as well as larger more traditional rental car giants like Avis and Hertz. Tesla’s welcome mat to fleet operators could encourage more small players to open up shop — helping the company saturate markets faster. TechCrunch
Startup ARR is less secure than ever, new research shows
2026-09-03

Startup ARR is less secure than ever, new research shows

TechCrunch | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
AI has ushered in a lot of never-happened-before moments, but one of the most transformative is its impact on enterprise IT. Companies that have historically been cautious and committed long-term to what they buy are on pace to spend $4.25 trillion on technology in 2026, market researcher IDC predicts. It’s almost all driven by AI. New research from venture capital firm Madrona shows that 74% of 150 enterprise IT professionals it surveyed plan to expand their AI budgets in the next 12 months, and the rest plan to hold spending steady. Yet these same enterprises say that fewer than half of their AI pilots ever make it into full production. That’s actually an improvement. Last year, MIT famously reported that 95% of enterprise AI projects had failed in terms of ROI. Fewer than half succeeding is a pretty low bar, but it’s better than a 5% success rate. But the most telling finding from Madrona’s report is that, even when an enterprise does roll out the AI tech, it doesn’t commit to it long term. Some 77% of enterprises reevaluate their AI vendors every six months or even on a rolling basis. “This creates a ‘fast in, fast out’ dynamic that is fundamentally different from traditional enterprise SaaS, where multi-year contracts provided a moat of inertia,” Madrona writes in the report. “In enterprise AI, switching costs are lower and the re-evaluation cadence is relentless.” This has widespread implications for all those fast-growing annual recurring revenue (ARR) numbers startups report. Enterprise trial budgets are what fueled the initial AI boom of 2025. This year was supposed to be the year these big customers settled in and started committing long term to AI startups. Enterprise contracts are what allow so many AI startups to claim astronomically fast revenue growth — think the phenomenon of startups going from $0-$10 million in three months. Yet, for the first time ever, enterprise revenue remains insecure, even after a startup’s AI product graduates out of a pilot phase and gets adopted by a company. Part of the issue is that many AI startups haven’t fully landed on a good way to price their AI wares for enterprises. New research from VC firm Andreessen Horowitz that surveyed 50 technical AI buyers found that more than half of them want AI fees tied to the work produced or other outcomes, rather than to usage like the number of tokens consumed. Charging for usage like tokens is basically a SaaS-era business model. Once an enterprise knows it needs email, or HR software, or cloud storage, it’s merely a matter of how many employees or how much data it must pay for. For AI, pricing “around the recognizable work” is what helps the startup prove its worth to the customer. When the fees revolve around, say, how many reports are processed, or tickets closed, or leads generated, this makes the product “economically valuable to both sides,” writes a16z partners Tugce Erten and Sarah Wang. All of this means that AI has potentially ushered in a new era of enterprise experimentation. That opens doors to startups — enterprises are more willing to try their tech — but it also means an enterprise contract no longer secures long-term revenue. When or if enterprises will revert to their long-term buying habits remains to be seen. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. TechCrunch
The Cybercab is Tesla’s ‘fork in the road’ moment
2026-09-03

The Cybercab is Tesla’s ‘fork in the road’ moment

TechCrunch | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
Today could be the day Tesla changes forever. In a couple of hours, Tesla will “launch” the Cybercab — a small, gold, two-seater sedan with no steering wheel or pedals that uses cameras and AI to navigate the world — in its hometown of Austin, Texas. The company hasn’t said what the launch will entail, exactly. It could be a few cars offering rides in a small area downtown, or a far grander affair in both size and scope. Tesla certainly wants you to believe it’s the latter. In the runup to the event, Tesla staged dozens — perhaps more — of its Cybercabs around Austin and in many other U.S. cities. At the time this story was published, 420 autonomous vehicles were registered in Texas, according to the state’s automated vehicle tracker. Ashok Elluswamy, the head of Tesla AI, recently said in a post on X that the “streets won’t be the same anymore.” When another user wrote that Tesla is “about to flood Austin,” CEO Elon Musk replied: “Yes.” On Wednesday night, Musk posted a seemingly AI-generated photo of the Cybercab consuming the city alongside a massive dust storm. “A Storm of Cybercabs,” Musk wrote in another post. These gravitas-laced posts mark a departure from the company’s go-slow-and-safe messaging in the months leading up to the event. Musk and other Tesla executives had previously professed they were taking a cautious approach to its robotaxis in the name of safety, though at times they seemed more concerned about public perception of a potential failure. Of course, Tesla’s launch strategy isn’t as meaningful as the outcome. Tesla’s ability to successfully build a robotaxi network around the Cybercab would provide the first tangible evidence that it has transitioned from an automaker with some side projects to an AI and robotics company. There’s a trope Musk favors that is apt for this moment: the fork in the road. It was the subject of an email he sent to Twitter employees in 2022 when he gave them an ultimatum to stay or leave. He invoked it again in early 2025 when he encouraged federal government employees to leave as part of his job leading DOGE, before he truly started wrecking house. Musk loves the metaphor so much that he commissioned a 30-foot-tall sculpture of a literal fork on a literal road for Burning Man in 2022, before moving the sculpture to a Tesla office. The Cybercab is Tesla’s fork in the road. This is either the moment the company begins to transform urban transportation as we know it, or the moment that reveals that, despite its many efforts, Tesla really is just an automaker after all. Musk first revealed the Cybercab in 2024 at a Hollywood movie lot. But the idea has been around far longer. Musk has spent more than a decade promising that Tesla would find a way to make its cars fully autonomous, paving the way for a network of robotaxis. He even went so far as to claim, in 2016, that every Tesla being made had the capability to become fully autonomous and that the cars only needed the right software update to make it happen. He was wrong. Tesla has reworked the hardware in its cars multiple times since that declaration, and Musk recently admitted that millions of them will need some kind of hardware retrofit to become autonomous. Still, Tesla kept developing driving automation software. It has steadily released and refined its primary offering, Full Self-Driving (Supervised). But as capable as that software has become, it is still driver-assistance software. The responsibility and liability remain with the driver. The Cybercab is supposed to change all that. It emerged from an effort by Tesla to develop a next-generation EV platform that was cheaper to build. The plan was for the company to take what it learned from the Model 3 and Model Y — its two biggest mass-market successes — and develop a new platform that would cost half as much, allowing Tesla to make more affordable EVs. Years of reporting and Musk’s own biography have shown that he was so dead set on achieving full autonomy that he decided against building a manually drivable car on the platform. Instead, Musk focused entirely on using the new platform to power the two-seater robotaxi we now know as the Cybercab. In April 2024, months ahead of the Cybercab reveal, he approved massive layoffs at Tesla in service of going “balls to the wall for autonomy,” which he called a “blindingly obvious move.” “Everything else is like variations on a horse carriage,” he wrote at the time. Tesla started testing the first version of its Robotaxi program last June in Austin using modified Model Y SUVs. For the first few months, the company had an employee in the front seat (sometimes behind the wheel, sometimes in the passenger seat) who could stop the car if something went wrong. Things have gone wrong along the way, though not catastrophically. Tesla has reported a few dozen incidents in Austin since it launched the Robotaxi pilot. Most were minor crashes with stationary objects, and a few involved teleoperators remotely moving the vehicles at low speeds. The company has expanded this Model Y-based system to a few other cities in Texas and Florida. But over the past year, the network has not lived up to the hype Tesla manufactured at launch in June 2025. In July, the company revealed that the number of paid miles the network was facilitating was actually moving backward. But that may not matter much to Tesla, which believes scaling its robotaxi network requires the Cybercab. Tesla designed the Cybercab around cost. It’s smaller, lighter, and has a lower-capacity battery pack than any other Tesla. Fewer raw materials — especially on the battery side — make it cheaper to build. The cheaper it is to build, the faster Tesla can potentially turn a profit once it’s deployed. That math has Tesla’s most ardent fans giddy. They look at Tesla’s ability to build dozens of Cybercabs for roughly the same amount of money Waymo spends to purchase and outfit fewer than 10 of its newest “Ojai” vans and see it as proof that the robotaxi war is already won. Musk seems to agree. In a reply to a post that included a graphical representation of this argument, he wrote, “this doesn’t even take into account the operational efficiency of Cybercab.” There are caveats worth considering. Tesla has, to date, only operated its robotaxi network within a geofence in each city. While some are larger than others, and the company has kept expanding some of them, Musk himself once said: “If you need a geofenced area, you don’t have real self-driving.” Then there’s the challenge of operating the network at scale. Tesla has so far benefited from having a small network in just a few cities. That naturally means its self-driving software encounters fewer edge cases than Waymo, which has 4,000 robotaxis on the road. Fewer trips and fewer edge cases also mean fewer chances for riders to spot problems and post them on social media or report them to regulators or journalists. Tesla has also benefited from the fact that its fleet has consisted of Model Y SUVs, which are harder to distinguish on the road than a bright white Jaguar I-Pace kitted out with sensors. That will change with the Cybercabs. If Tesla’s software isn’t up to the task, its failures will be much harder to miss when they happen in shiny gold cars. Most recently, he was a reporter at Bloomberg News where he helped break stories about some of the most notorious EV SPAC flops. Why You Shouldn’t Work on Labor Day
2026-09-04

Why You Shouldn’t Work on Labor Day

TIME | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings It’s Saturday morning and your boss emails you a question that can wait until Monday. What do you do? If you’re like me, you read the message while walking out of your yoga class and respond before lunch. This work culture makes the U.S. an outlier among high-income economies. Since 2017, countries including France, Belgium, Spain, Portugal, Ireland, Australia, Slovakia, Greece, and Argentina have legislated some form of a right to disconnect. The European Union is working on a policy that could take effect as soon as 2028. In Australia, employees have the right not to read or respond to employers outside of work hours. In Portugal, mid-size employers risk fines of over $10,000 for contacting employees when they’re off the clock. As we think about the employment landscape in the U.S. this Labor Day, we should consider a similar approach that protects the right of employees to not work when they’re not officially working. This could solve a lot of problems for families and communities while actually improving job performance. Since the pandemic, the boundaries between work and home have all but collapsed. With many workers still doing a lot of remote work, many of us send our first email early in the morning and our last late at night, creating what Microsoft calls the “infinite workday.” We squeeze in a load of laundry, parent-teacher conference, and family dinner somewhere in between. Being pulled in so many directions at the same time is awful for our health and the quality of our work, for a simple reason: The human brain can’t multitask. “Each time we are exposed to a new stimulus and engage in the sequential process of disconnecting and reallocating our attention, we are expending valuable metabolic energy,” University of California, Santa Barbara technology management professor Paul Leonardi writes in Digital Exhaustion: Simple Rules for Reclaiming Your Life. “Our attention is fragmented. We lose focus and become easily distracted. We can’t get things done as quickly or as easily as we should be able to, and often quality suffers.” Humans just didn’t evolve to keep switching between tasks as quickly and regularly as we do today, Leonardi warns. This explains why so many of us are exhausted and burned out. It also explains why we spend so much time working: We aren’t as efficient as we could be. When I don’t work on the weekend, I unquestionably show up as a better employee on Monday morning. That’s because spending time with friends and family, exercising, getting outdoors, and volunteering in my community leave me refreshed and energized. What’s more, when I get my kids’ permission slips filled out and the fruit and veggies chopped on the weekend, thoughts of unfinished business aren’t floating through my head while I’m working. That means I can achieve a state of what the psychologist Mihaly Csikszentmihalyi famously termed “flow,” getting completely absorbed in my work and reaching peak performance. Allowing workers to disconnect would therefore make them better employees. It could also solve several public policy problems. Our children need present caregivers. It only takes seconds for a child to fall or drown while we’re distracted by a manager’s latest missive. When parents spend more time on screens, their children also develop poorer language skills. And, of course, children are unlikely to open up about their problems while we’re distracted on our phones. Plus, if workers disconnected during their off-hours, they’d also have more time to nurture the offline connections that our country desperately needs. I would agree that we’re experiencing what psychologist Marisa Franco calls a “friendship famine.” Since 1990, the percentage of American adults who say they have no close friends has quadrupled, according to last year’s American Perspectives Survey. That is crushing. The quality of our friendships and social connections is one of the strongest predictors of our health, happiness, and how long we’ll live, according to the Harvard Study of Adult Development. It’s more important than things like our cholesterol, genes, and salaries. And being lonely is as toxic for our health as alcoholism or smoking. Of course, as Americans know one another less, we help one another less. This can explain why volunteerism has also been declining. That’s particularly sad because banding together has long been a central part of our national character, as Alexis de Tocqueville famously wrote in Democracy in America. It’s especially important in a country like ours where people can’t rely on a strong social safety net when they hit hard times. Labor Day is an obvious prompt for U.S. lawmakers to consider a right to disconnect. In the meantime, we can change our behavior and our workplaces. Employers should take the lead. Even without a national law, they should develop their own policies about disconnecting and publish them prominently on their websites so prospective employees can consider them even before accepting job offers. Volkswagen pioneered this approach in Germany in 2012 when it stopped sending emails to employees who weren’t senior managers 30 minutes after their shifts ended and resumed 30 minutes before their next shifts began. A series of studies published in 2021 found that when people receive non-urgent work emails outside of work hours, we tend to think we need to respond faster than the senders believe is necessary. Receiving these types of emails also increases our stress and decreases our wellbeing, but senders underestimate the degree to which their messages stress us out. Indicating in the email that a response wasn’t expected right away reduced the pressure on recipients. An even better strategy could be schedule-sending emails so colleagues receive them when they return to work. Another thing we can combat individually is the urge to check our messages every time we have a spare minute. Pulling out our phones is often a convenient way to avoid negative emotions or awkward encounters. Yet all this task switching leaves us distracted and depleted, and I can’t realistically handle any of the issues in the emails I read in the 30 seconds it takes to walk from my yoga class to my car. It stands to reason that our brains would be less foggy if we used those small slices of our lives to take a deep breath, notice our surroundings, and have short, pleasant interactions with the people around us. When we returned to our desks, we’d think more clearly, work more efficiently, and send better responses. This Labor Day, there’s a way for workers to be healthier, happier, more productive, and solve a lot of our public policy problems, too. We need to stop working when we’re not working. Earle Meets World Is One Long ‘Get Ready With Me’ Video
2026-09-04

Earle Meets World Is One Long ‘Get Ready With Me’ Video

TIME | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings “You’re gonna see everything on this show,” Alix Earle assures viewers in the voiceover that opens her Netflix reality series Earle Meets World. But if the show made a genuine effort to fulfill the 25-year-old social media star’s vow to reveal the rawest, most intimate aspects of her life, then the only reasonable conclusion to draw from it would be that there just isn’t much to see. As her fans (she has 8.5 million on TikTok alone) will anticipate, “everything” refers specifically to ongoing drama around Earle’s parents’ divorce, her own breakup with NFL player Braxton Berrios, and her mysterious falling-out with fellow creator and former boss Alex Cooper. All of this does indeed come up in the eight-episode series, now streaming in full, which tries mightily to position the large, blended Earle family as the Kardashian-Jenners for a new generation. Yet the bombshells teased in months’ worth of promotional materials aren’t especially explosive. Nor is there a breakout personality among a cast that, understandably enough, seems either too chill or too concerned with appearing likable to really entertain. Earle Meets World plays less like a juicy docusoap than like a tediously extended version of the Get Ready With Me (GRWM) videos that made its subject famous—one that suggests less overlap than you might expect between the qualities of a successful influencer and those required for reality TV stardom. Earle is one of those celebrities who are universally known to people under 30 but barely on the radar of anyone much older. (I’ll admit to having confused her with Cooper, another all-American blonde who parlayed internet fame into a Netflix series, and I know I’m not the only one.) Though I wouldn’t recommend making the show your first trip down the Alix Earle rabbit hole, here is a quick intro for the unfamiliar: Conventionally beautiful yet plagued by cystic acne, Earle grew her following as a University of Miami undergrad with mirror-facing GRWM TikToks, eventually going viral with a vulnerable post about her skin troubles. Then came the brand deals and new platforms. Cooper signed Earle, an obvious heir, to her podcast network; they abruptly parted ways in early 2025 and have been vaguely but publicly feuding ever since. Meanwhile, Earle has continued her ascent, launching a skincare brand, Reale Actives, and introducing herself to people elderly enough to watch network TV on Dancing With the Stars. From left: Sally, Alix Earle, and Ashtin Earle in Earle Meets World Netflix Synergistically positioned to bring her social media followers to a streaming platform that needs young subscribers to maintain its dominance while filling gaps in her own reach, Earle Meets World is a multigenerational family soap. Alix and 23-year-old Ashtin—a less-extroverted brunette whose TikTok audience still tops a million—are the It-girl sisters who constantly squabble but consider each other best friends. Their mom, Alisa, and dad, TJ, have been divorced for more than a decade, their split catalyzed by TJ’s affair with Ashley Dupré, a key character in the prostitution scandal that sank former New York governor Eliot Spitzer. Now Dupré is Ashley Earle, TJ’s wife and the mother of their three kids; the eldest, Izabel, is, at 13, already cranking out Outfit of the Day videos like the Gen Alpha Kylie Jenner she has the pedigree to become. A recent breast cancer survivor, Alisa has her own longtime partner, Todd. Her messy separation from TJ has evidently given way to a tenuous friendship between the couples, although it seems doubtful they spend as much time together off-camera as on. These relationships are further complicated by the role TJ, who made his fortune in construction, has assumed as Alix and Ashtin’s “dadager.” All the raw material for an unscripted family drama is present. Somehow, though, the fireworks tend to fizzle as soon as they’re lit. With the divorce so far in the past, a season’s worth of halfhearted conflict among the parents is wrung out of TJ joking that Alisa could be a “young piece of ass” on The Golden Bachelor. Ashley’s friction with Alix is framed as fallout from the latter’s history of oversharing about the former but feels more like standard stepfamily tension, heightened by reality-TV producers' prodding and Alix’s hypervigilance about her stepmom’s sometimes-awkward social media presence. Ironically, we only really witness the self-centered, control-freak behavior relatives are always attributing to Alix when she’s struggling to manage her image and brand identity. As a result, the most intense moments of the series tend to involve cringey TikToks or premature glimpses of Reale Actives packaging. You probably have to be a diehard Earle girl to find this stuff compelling. And even if you are, you might be disappointed by how many climactic Earle Meets World scenes, from Ashtin kicking Alix out of her New York apartment to Cooper goading Alix to come at her, unfold in now-ancient vertical videos that already have millions of views. It doesn’t help the show that the two people with whom Alix seems most preoccupied throughout the season—Cooper and Berrios—aren’t present. Instead, we get her side of these stories, conveyed in interviews and stiff conversations over cocktails at trendy, jewel-toned lounges, often so stripped of details that they come off as, respectively, a generic exploitative mentorship and your typical ambivalent breakup. A scene in the premiere that seems even more staged than the average docusoap grievance dump has Alix talking with TJ about Cooper in sentences that could’ve been scripted by a crisis-PR pro: “I definitely feel betrayed by Alex Cooper.” “She took advantage of my admiration for her.” Neither this storyline nor the Berrios one advances until the very end of the season, and even those late developments are anticlimactic. (Tabloid headlines linking Earle and Tom Brady, meanwhile, get coy winks but no substantive context.) From left: Ashley Earle, Thomas "TJ" Earle, Alisa Earle, and Todd in Earle Meets World Netflix Much of the blame for Earle Meets World’s slightness and superficiality, as well as the shapelessness of episodes that drag despite running less than 40 minutes apiece, should fall on the show’s producers. Not only have they failed to identify entertaining narratives within this family’s daily life; they’ve also made their desperation to do so too apparent in the final product. To be fair, they had a difficult assignment. As eager as they look to be on screen together, the Earles are—or act—too calm and conciliatory to drive reality-soap plots. No one wants to look unhinged, least of all Alix, who talks about being unstable but can’t risk her credibility as an entrepreneur, pitchwoman, and parasocial companion to millions by getting truly ugly on camera. Which is precisely what natural born reality stars have always done. Compare Alix and Ashtin’s nights at the club to the sloppy-drunk violence and infidelity of the 20-somethings on Jersey Shore and Vanderpump Rules, or her parents’ relationships to chaotic Real Housewives marriages, and it all looks rather tame. No one here can quip like the best Bravo scrappers and Traitors debaters, either, so the show lacks the element of humor that suffuses every classic of the genre. Influencers fall into a cultural gray area between entertainment and entrepreneurship; most excel at one more than the other. Both fields demand storytelling skills. But that doesn’t mean a person with a preternatural ability to sell people the products they watch her apply to her face as she scatters breadcrumbs about her personal life necessarily has the makings of an unscripted drama’s protagonist. Earle might be too shrewd a businesswoman to have unadulterated main character energy.

Breaking Down the Stunning Finale of Silo Season 3

Breaking Down the Stunning Finale of Silo Season 3

TIME | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Rebecca Ferguson in the Silo Season 3 finale Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Following the stunning final scene of Season 2 of Silo, the third season has diverged into two timelines: one with Juliette (Rebecca Ferguson) and company in Silo 18 fighting for their survival, and another unfolding before the silo program began. The season’s penultimate episode delivered some big revelations: The all-knowing Algorithm program that ensures the survival of the silo isn’t actually a digital algorithm, but a person watching from Silo 1, deciding everyone’s fates. Before he dies in Episode 9, Bernard (Tim Robbins) offers some hope: “A person can be defeated.” Meanwhile, in the past timeline, Daniel (Ashley Zukerman) and Helen (Jessica Henwick) discover the origins of the silos, only for a shocking nuclear explosion to separate them. That’s a hell of a way to end an episode, and the Season 3 finale of Silo, “Troy,” brings us straight into Silo 1, where those running the system live, far in the future. How far, exactly? “Three hundred and fifty years,” says series creator Graham Yost. “It’s revealed in Episode 9 that the silos were part of a 500-year plan. And they're 350 years into that.” Henwick and Zukerman in Episode 9 Courtesy of Apple TV Inside Silo 1, we see Daniel waking from cryo-sleep. Except he’s no longer called Daniel: He is now Troy. And while Troy looks physically the same as Daniel, he’s completely changed. That’s made crystal clear when he settles into his shift and has to deal with the outbreak in Silo 17, the same silo Juliette ventured to in Season 2. Troy, without hesitation, delivers a ruthless decision to execute every single person—man, woman, and child—who left the silo. A top priority of Silo 1 is to prevent silos from discovering that any other silos exist. His decision to unleash drone warfare is met with disapproval by drone pilot Susan, who is actually Daniel’s sister Charlotte (Jessica Brown Findlay). Neither of them recognizes one another from the past. “He’s completely bloodless about it. Totally unemotional. It’s just his job,” says Yost. (Yost does suggest that Charlotte’s negative reaction to Troy’s behavior is worth our attention, though he won’t say why). That lack of emotion bears no resemblance to the past Daniel. It turns out there’s a system designed to keep memories suppressed. Yost divulges some key details behind this Silo 1 initiative. “Your skill set, your skill knowledge, your expertise stays with you, and so that's why Daniel, as an engineer, is someone who can solve problems. There is an effort to wipe and control memory, so people don't know who they were. But they know who they became in the project.” Yost says we’ll discover more about specifics next season. It’s precisely the same idea the Algorithm pushed Camille to implement in Silo 18, a major plot point of Season 3. If she had done it, the silo would have continued to function, but there would have been huge problems. “People would forget they had kids and not feed them, and people would forget they were married to each other,” says Yost. The memory suppression plotline is influenced by a book by British neurologist Oliver Sacks. “In one of his books, a guy has extraordinary short-term memory loss. He’ll say ‘Hello, nice to meet you’ every 15 seconds or so. But if he sits down at the piano, he can play an entire concerto. So there's something about that persistence of memory; that part of his brain keeps going. We modeled it on that.” Jessica Brown Findlay and Matt Craven in the first episode of Season 3 Courtesy of Apple TV We also discover that Daniel reports to Victor (Matt Craven), whom audiences first met as Charlotte’s doctor at the beginning of the season and who is one of the leading minds behind the silo program. Further, we see that Victor is actually the voice behind the almighty Algorithm. Victor seems deeply disturbed by Troy’s willingness to massacre the people of silo. Interestingly, he does not have a different name from the one he had before—and neither does the silo director, Senator Rosalind Thurman (Laura Innes). They have maintained their identities, and unlike Daniel/Troy, they know everything that’s happened in the last 350 years. “Power dictates who is in power, and that power gets to retain memory. But there’s a burden in retaining memory,” Yost says. Troy comes to Victor with a request. He’s pestered by visions of a woman, who we know is Helen. These flashbacks play on his mind, and he hopes that Victor can help him find out who she is. “The whole purpose and design of the project is to free us from the past,” Victor tells Troy, gently brushing off his request. Victor makes a note of it before Troy goes back to cryo-sleep until his next shift. It’s revealed that Troy has been asking Victor for information about Helen ever since he became part of the silo over 300 years ago, as we see a folder full of the times he’s asked about her. With Troy back in cryo-sleep, some time later, Victor has a new problem to deal with: the uprising in Silo 18. Victor has put the dreaded safeguard in motion, a fail-safe created by the silo founders to annihilate an entire silo’s population if rebellion or a discovery of knowledge threatens the entirety of the silo system. Camille (Alexandria Riley), Silo 18’s head of IT, now armed with the knowledge that the Algorithm is human, manages to outsmart it, providing a distraction to stop the safeguard by capping the concealed pipe that would flood the silo with lethal poison gas. It’s a monumental success for Silo 18, and a crushing blow for Silo 1, one that could threaten to destroy the entire system and everything that’s been building for over 300 years. And yet, Victor’s face does not register the disappointment we might expect. “He felt relieved that Silo 18 didn’t die,” says Yost. It’s a surprise, but given what follows, it makes plenty of sense. Victor ends up taking his own life. “We never land on exactly why he killed himself, but there are a lot of different reasons, says Yost. “It's a constellation, more than one trigger. But I think that for him, a big trigger was seeing Daniel, this guy that he quite liked, do this horrific thing. It's like, ‘Is that what we are? Is that what we've done?’ I think when he realized he was actually happy that 18 survived, he knew he wasn't on board with this anymore.” Ferguson in Juliette in the penultimate episode Courtesy of Apple TV Thirty-five years later, Troy is awoken from cryo-sleep again, and learns that Victor is no longer alive. Troy is to take over as the Algorithm, and faces off against Juliette, who’s approached the giant door at the bottom of the Silo 18. In Silo 18, that door is referred to as “the forward vault,” suggesting that the door is actually a path to another silo. This means that the massacre of Silo 17 happened approximately 35 years before we met Juliette for the first time in Silo Season 1. As Juliette and friends approach the door, the Algorithm (a.k.a. Troy) communicates with them. They strike a deal. If Juliette agrees to stop investigating, Silo 1 won’t attempt any further safeguarding, leaving them in peace. Juliette accepts the offer, but she doesn’t mean it. Troy threatened to kill everyone in the silo should Juliette not agree, and she believes it’s only a matter of time before they try and kill everyone again.Juliette has the genesis of a plan. “They’re expecting us to defend ourselves, which means they won’t be preparing to defend against us. If we can get to them first, we can protect everyone. We need to take out Silo 1” she declares. Before the season ends, Troy finds an encrypted message from Victor. The message threatens to undo 350 years of memory wiping: “Her name is Helen Drew,” Victor reveals to Troy. We close in on Troy’s face as tears fall down his cheeks as he says Helen’s name out loud, and the episode cuts to black. Ashley Zukerman and Jessica Henwick Courtesy of Apple TV “The big things we wanted to set up at the end of the season were Juliette saying we have to take down Silo 1, and Daniel saying Helen’s name,” says Yost. These events seem to set up a Season 4 battle between Troy and Juliette, one that could topple the entire silo system. But Troy’s realization seems to suggest that he may in fact revert to Daniel, which could mean that he will instead begin to work against Silo 1, opening a possibility of teaming up with Juliette to take the silos down for good. Yost, of course, will not reveal if this, or anything like it, will happen in Season 4, the final season of Silo, currently scheduled for sometime next summer. But there is one thing Yost will say about the upcoming struggle between Silo 1 and Silo 18:

How Americans and Canadians Are Feeling About the Escalating Trade War
2026-09-03

How Americans and Canadians Are Feeling About the Escalating Trade War

TIME | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings American President Donald Trump greets Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney during a summit on Gaza in Sharm el-Sheikh on Oct. 13, 2025. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings With relations between the U.S. and Canada becoming more and more strained following the breakdown in trade talks between the two countries, most Canadians are backing their government and the way it has chosen to handle the standoff. But on the other side of the border, Americans are offering the Trump Administration far less support for its approach. Trade negotiations between the U.S. and Canada collapsed last month, with each side placing the blame on the other. Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney criticized the U.S. for requesting what he described as “last-minute changes” that he argued were “unfair, uneconomic, and called into question the reliability of any deal.” American President Donald Trump, meanwhile, accused Canada of “‘Ripping Off’ the U.S.A. for decades” and portrayed the country as being “difficult and unreasonable.” The day after trade talks broke down, the Trump Administration’s 50% tariffs on billions of dollars worth of Canadian products took effect. Canada then announced retaliatory tariffs that will go into effect on Sept. 8. Tensions between the two nations have only continued to grow. Last week, Trump signed an Executive Order directing his Interior Secretary to change the name of Lake Ontario to “Lake America”—a move that angered Canadians and sparked pushback from political leaders in both Canada and the U.S. There is no clear resolution in sight for the rift in the countries’ critical trading relationship, with both Trump and Carney remaining firm in their stances. While speaking to reporters on Tuesday, Carney signaled that he would be willing to resume negotiations—but only if U.S. officials stop insulting and mocking his country. “When the Americans stop doing memes, stop throwing shade, stop trying to be tough, and start being serious about having those discussions, we can have those discussions,” the Canadian leader said. “It’s not constructive,” he added. “But that’s their democracy.” Trump, meanwhile, doubled down on his accusations against Canada when asked the following day about the prospect of talks starting back up. “Canada has been ripping off the United States for many, many years, for decades, many decades,” the President said during a Wednesday press conference. Here’s what recent polls reveal about how Americans and Canadians feel about the heated situation—and how their leaders are handling it. Most Americans—nearly 70%—said they feel that the U.S. should be open to reaching a compromise with Canada, even if that leads to many of the U.S.’s demands not being met, according to an Ipsos poll that was released on Tuesday. In comparison, only 25% of Americans said they believe that the U.S. should take a firm stand against its northern neighbor and push for many of its demands to be met. Americans were also more likely to hold their own government responsible for the breakdown in relations than Canada’s. About 46% said they think that the U.S. shoulders more of the blame for the ongoing trade war, while only 12% said they believe that Canada is more culpable and 18% said they feel that both parties are equally at fault, according to the poll. The survey also found that most Americans oppose actions that could or have already escalated tensions between the two countries. For instance, nearly 60% of Americans said they are against the U.S. imposing additional tariffs on Canada, while only 20% indicated support for such an aggressive step. And more than 60% of Americans said they disagree with Trump’s order to rename Lake Ontario, while only 14% backed the move. Americans’ objection to the Executive Order in particular was seen across party lines—84% of Democrats and 65% of independents said they were against it, and more Republicans (43%) objected to it than those who agreed with it (33%). A recent Economist/YouGov poll produced similar results: nearly 60% of Americans who responded to the survey said they were against increasing tariffs on Canadian products, compared to less than 30% who said they would back such a move. About 45% said they think Canada has not been unfair to the U.S. when it comes to trading between the two nations, while just 28% are aligned with Trump in thinking that Canada has been unfair. The majority—64%—of Americans still describe Canada as an ally or friendly, according to the Economist/YouGov poll. But while they remain a minority, the portion of Americans who view Canada in a negative light has grown in recent years; about 22% describe Canada as either unfriendly or an enemy, a record high since 2016. That growth has mostly been driven by Americans who identify as Republicans, the survey found. Amid the standoff with Canada, Trump’s approval rating has remained at around 38%, where it has hovered in recent months. That figure marks a stark 14-point drop from where his approval rating stood when he retook the White House last January. The majority of Canadians—63%—said they feel that the country made the right choice to maintain its stance and walk away from trade talks with the U.S., “even if it meant higher costs and job losses,” according to an Ipsos poll conducted for Global News that was released on Aug. 29. Even more Canadians—73%—said they agreed with the nation’s decision to launch retaliatory tariffs on American products worth billions of dollars. The poll did find, though, that support for those actions was lower among younger demographics than it was among older age groups. For instance, a little less than 50% of Gen Z adults backed Canada’s decision to remain firm in trade negotiations, compared to 80% for elderly boomers. Another poll, conducted by the Canadian nonprofit Angus Reid Institute, found that about 76% of Canadians believe the country was right to suspend trade talks, and that 69% said they believe that Carney demonstrated “strength” by turning down a “bad deal.” A majority of Canadians—62%—also indicated support for retaliatory tariffs against the U.S., calling the move “about right under the circumstances.” Still, 89% of Canadians expressed concerns that the trade war with the U.S. would affect prices, the poll found—though more than 60% of Canadians said they think the country will eventually come out of the dispute stronger. As Trump’s approval rating has remained underwater, the Carney government’s increased by four points to 56% in recent weeks, while its disapproval rating has dropped two points to just 29%, according to data shared by the polling firm Abacus Data on Sunday. Approval for Carney individually has also improved recently, with 53% of Canadians having a positive opinion of him and only 28% having a negative view of him. The polling firm said that its findings indicate that “the Carney government’s political standing is built primarily on how Canadians judge its management of the American relationship.” The Canadian Liberal Party, which Carney is the leader of, has preserved its narrow majority in Parliament, with its candidates emerging victorious in three special elections earlier this week. Carney cited his government’s actions in the trade war as a driving reason for the victories. “What’s a clear message from last night in three very different ridings across this great diverse country is that Canadians are behind the plan of the government, which is first and foremost about building strong at home, diversifying with reliable partners, building Canada strong for all,” he said on Tuesday.
