‘I Wouldn’t Call it a War’: Vance Downplays Conflict With Iran, Even as Strikes Continue
2026-09-03

‘I Wouldn’t Call it a War’: Vance Downplays Conflict With Iran, Even as Strikes Continue

TIME | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Vice President JD Vance speaks at the first press briefing the White House has held in over 40 days, on September 03, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Vice President J.D. Vance said Thursday that he would not characterize the United States as being at war with Iran, even as the two countries have exchanged strikes in recent days and the Trump Administration has declined to say when a conflict now stretching beyond six months might finally end. “I wouldn’t call it a war,” Vance told reporters at a White House press briefing, the first since Karoline Leavitt stepped down as press secretary last month. “Right now, there is no active shooting.” The distinction was striking after a renewed burst of hostilities this week, including American strikes inside Iran and Iranian attacks in the region. But it echoed an argument that President Trump and other Republicans made in the earliest days of the conflict: that the United States was carrying out a limited military operation rather than embarking on another prolonged Middle Eastern war, which, under the Constitution, would require congressional approval. More than six months later, that distinction has become increasingly difficult to sustain. The United States has repeatedly bombed targets inside Iran as Tehran attacks commercial shipping vessels through the Strait of Hormuz, contributing to higher energy prices. Vance acknowledged what he called a recent “flare-up” but argued that the most intensive phase of the American campaign had ended long ago. “Major combat operations are currently not ongoing, and they haven’t been ongoing in a very long time,” he said. He said Operation Epic Fury, the large-scale campaign that followed last year’s Operation Midnight Hammer, lasted roughly six weeks. The earlier operation, he said, destroyed Iranian nuclear facilities, while Epic Fury severely damaged Iran’s conventional military and its ability to produce weapons and rebuild its nuclear program. But Vance offered little indication of when the broader confrontation might end, saying the Administration would not commit itself to a timeline while Iran continued threatening commercial shipping and global energy supplies. Asked whether the conflict would be over before November’s midterm elections, Vance said he did not know. “You would have to ask the Iranians,” he said, adding: “When will the Iranians stop shooting at ships?” The answer underscored the open-ended nature of a conflict that Trump and others in the Administration repeatedly predicted would be brief. As recently as Wednesday, the President said he did not believe it would last “much longer.” But his Administration is simultaneously intensifying economic pressure on Iran, maintaining a naval blockade, and reserving the option of further military strikes if Iran attacks American interests or interferes with commercial shipping. Presidents have long resisted the “war” label in conflicts undertaken without a formal declaration of war. Harry Truman called the American intervention in Korea a United Nations “police action,” while Bill Clinton avoided describing NATO’s bombing campaign in Kosovo as a war. Barack Obama similarly argued that the 2011 intervention in Libya did not constitute a “war” requiring congressional authorization. In March, Trump openly acknowledged that referring to the conflict as “war” carried legal significance because Congress had not authorized it. “I won’t use the word ‘war’ because they say, if you use the word war, that’s maybe not a good thing to do,” he told House Republicans at the time. “They don’t like the word ‘war,’ because you’re supposed to get approval, so I’ll use the word ‘military operation,’ which is really what it is.” House Speaker Mike Johnson similarly insisted shortly after the initial strikes that the country was “not at war,” describing the campaign instead as “a very specific, clear mission.” Democrats have rejected that characterization and repeatedly tried to force votes requiring congressional authorization for continued hostilities. Those efforts have failed largely along party lines. The dispute has revived a decades-old argument over presidential war powers and the extent to which a president can sustain military operations abroad without explicit approval from Congress. Vance said Thursday that the administration would not rely on Iran to negotiate in good faith and would instead use whatever tools it considered necessary to prevent Iran from obtaining a nuclear weapon and to protect global energy markets. He argued that Iran’s grip on the Strait of Hormuz was “effectively gone” and that oil traffic through the crucial waterway was nearing its pre-conflict level. Shipping data, however, shows traffic remains well below where it stood before the fighting began. The Administration has made restoring commercial traffic through the strait a central objective as energy prices have become an increasing political liability ahead of the midterms. Vance acknowledged the pain of higher gasoline prices but argued that they could have risen substantially more without American intervention in Iran. “Gas, frankly, could have been much, much higher were it not for our efforts,” he said. He declined to predict when prices might return to around $3 a gallon, saying the more important objective was ensuring “that Iran doesn’t have a nuclear weapon.” The Administration is now pursuing what amounts to a strategy of intermittent military force combined with sustained economic pressure. Last week, it announced “Operation Economic Outcast,” an effort to further isolate Iran from its remaining trading partners. But the campaign has so far produced limited results, and imposing sanctions aggressive enough to cripple Iranian trade could require confronting countries including China, India, and Russia. Secretary of State Marco Rubio said this week that the Administration expected the principal pressure on Iran going forward to be economic, while reserving the right to use military force when necessary. Trump himself has resumed publicly encouraged Iranians to challenge the regime, writing on Truth Social on Tuesday, “When are the Iranian people going to rise up and fight?” When asked by TIME on Thursday whether the Trump Administration was considering arming Iranian dissidents or providing them with other direct assistance, Vance declined to provide more details. “Everything that could happen is on the table: economic pressure, military pressure, diplomatic pressure, covert pressure,” he said. “Of course, these are tools in the President's toolkit.” Iranian leaders, meanwhile, have shown little sign of capitulating. Its leadership remains divided between officials advocating negotiations and hard-liners pressing for confrontation, and Tehran has continued striking American interests and allies in response to U.S. attacks. The renewed American bombing has also brought fresh scrutiny of civilian casualties. Iranian authorities said a U.S. strike this week hit a wedding celebration in Sirik in southern Iran, killing at least five people and wounding scores more, including women and children. A New York Times visual analysis and a weapons expert concluded that the wedding was struck by a bomb released by American forces during attacks in the area. Vance said the episode was under investigation but expressed deep skepticism about Iran’s account. “The Iranian state media has not been a very good scribe about what’s happened in the conflict thus far,” he said. “So I’m extremely skeptical of this.” “Obviously, sometimes things happen,” he added. “When our military makes mistakes, they learn from them to try to get better from them, so we’re investigating it very fully.”

The Stakes of America’s 2026 Midterm Elections
2026-09-03

The Stakes of America’s 2026 Midterm Elections

TIME | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A sign outside a polling location during a primary election in Marblehead, Massachusetts, US, on Tuesday, Sept. 1, 2026. Five years ago, in the wake of the Jan. 6 attack on the Capitol, America’s civic institutions pledged to do more to defend our system of fair and free elections. Major philanthropies increased their investments in programs that defend democracy. Law firms expanded their pro bono election-protection work. University presidents publicly condemned the effort to thwart the will of American voters. Many of the country’s largest businesses pledged to stop donating to candidates who had tried to overturn an election. Today, our elections are again under attack. But while some institutions have maintained their commitment to defending democracy, far too many have retreated. There are fewer than 100 days until the 2026 nationwide elections. Now is the time for everyone to protect the system of fair and free elections upon which our society and prosperity ultimately depend. Together, the two of us have spent considerable time in all the major sectors of American society: philanthropy, business, academia, and the law. We understand that leaders in these sectors fear government retribution, and understandably so. But with the integrity of our elections under enormous strain—and with government officials signaling that more aggressive attacks may follow—it is time for all institutions to reaffirm their role as defenders of the free and fair elections at the heart of American democracy. Defending our democracy begins with a clear-eyed assessment of the threats to it. Over the past 18 months, the Justice Department has disbanded government agencies meant to protect elections from foreign interference and to help states administer them smoothly. President Donald Trump has repeatedly made false claims that the 2020 election was fraudulent and made new ones—without evidence—about the midterms. Secretary of Homeland Security Markwayne Mullin recently threatened to jail local election officials his department finds were insufficiently responsive to the federal government’s unproven claims of fraud.  Overall, the federal government has tried to wrest control of elections from the states, with courts repeatedly finding such efforts illegal. Now, experts across the country are deeply concerned about what might occur in the coming months, whether it’s false allegations that cast doubt on individual races, state officials refusing to certify election results as required by law, or baseless efforts to block House members from taking their seats in January. The good news is the success of these efforts to undermine the midterms is not inevitable. To the contrary, enormous work has already been done to protect the integrity of November’s election from those who would undermine it. Now, it is time for leaders who haven’t yet joined that effort to get off the sidelines no matter what their politics are. Losing our system of fair and free elections would not just affect one element of American life. It would affect all elements of American life. There are many ways for institutions to meet the moment and keep our country’s elections free and fair. First, many leaders, particularly those from the business community, meet regularly with members of the executive branch and Congress. They should use these relationships to remind elected officials of the need to respect election results no matter who wins or loses. These nonpartisan principles are the basic preconditions for a functioning democracy and should be respected by all. Second, many institutional leaders have strong ties to state and local officials. Now is the time to remind state legislators that attempting to disenfranchise eligible voters via last-minute changes to election rules, baseless claims of fraud, or partisan attempts to deny certification harm our democracy and create the kind of turmoil that negatively affects a state’s reputation and business climate. Third, leaders should forcefully support the local election administrators who run our elections. These are not professional politicians. They are our neighbors, co-workers, and friends—and recently, they have far too often been subject to threats and harassment by those who wish to undermine their nonpartisan mission. Institutional leaders can help elections run smoothly by encouraging their employees to become poll workers, and by supporting nonpartisan civic organizations that defend election officials in performing their duties, or that bring Americans from across the political spectrum together to support the right of all Americans to have their vote counted. Standing up for fair and free elections is not without risk. But with the foundation of our democracy under unprecedented assault and our basic rights on the line, the far greater risk is ignoring the threat to our country until it is too late. One day, leaders of America’s institutions will face the same profound question as every other citizen who loves this country. Future generations will ask, “Where were you when the stakes were highest?” TIME Ideas hosts the world's leading voices, providing commentary on events in news, society, and culture. We welcome outside contributions. Opinions expressed do not necessarily reflect the views of TIME editors.

Trump’s Antitrust Gambit May Backfire
2026-09-04

Trump’s Antitrust Gambit May Backfire

The Atlantic | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
In March, one of the most important antitrust cases in recent memory went to trial. The Department of Justice and 39 states plus the District of Columbia had sued the ticketing-and-entertainment giant Live Nation, alleging that its 2010 merger with Ticketmaster had created an effective monopoly on live shows. According to the suit, Live Nation controls 70 percent of what the government defined as “major concert venues” in the United States, and Ticketmaster handles 86 percent of concert ticketing at those venues. The DOJ accused the company of exploiting this monopoly by compelling venues to use Ticketmaster as their exclusive seller, jacking up prices, and saddling concertgoers with unavoidable junk fees. This certainly looked like an archetypal case of a company’s size leaving consumers worse off—precisely what our antitrust laws are designed to prevent. This is what made it so disappointing when, a week after the trial began, DOJ announced that it had reached a settlement with Live Nation. Although the agreement mandated changes to some of the company’s practices, including granting access to other booking agents at some venues and limiting fees, the deal fell well short of the more aggressive remedies that many people had hoped for, including—most obvious—breaking up the company. This odd turn of events makes sense only because we know now that the decision to settle was made not by anyone in DOJ’s Antitrust Division but by the president himself, according to a recent report in The Wall Street Journal. Just before the trial was set to begin, Donald Trump met with the CEO of Live Nation in the White House a couple of times and allegedly told a senior DOJ official, “Settle it.” In other words, the federal government abandoned one of its strongest antitrust lawsuits in years because Trump wanted it to. Quinta Jurecic: DOJ enters a new, even more aggressive phase This may then seem like yet another story about how Trump has wrecked norms and corrupted the rule of law. But in this case, there’s a twist. After the Department of Justice announced in March that it was settling with Live Nation, many observers expected that the states involved would drop the suit and sign on to the deal, as typically occurs when federal regulators reach a settlement. But in this case, 32 states—including Republican-led Florida, Tennessee, Texas, and Utah—and D.C. refused to settle. In April, they won their case: A jury found that Live Nation had illegally monopolized the live-events business and overcharged consumers. The judge is still determining the remedy, but it will almost certainly be tougher than the deal Live Nation got from DOJ. This will not be a one-off. A peculiarity of federal antitrust law is that state attorneys general can bring lawsuits on behalf of their citizens for violations of the Sherman Antitrust Act, and many states also have their own antitrust laws on the books. This gives them a great deal of leeway to act on their own. And because of the Trump administration’s attitude toward antitrust laws, what we saw in the Live Nation case—states refusing to defer to the feds, and instead pursuing litigation—will become more common. This is because Trump, despite having campaigned as a populist who would fight to lower prices, has presided over an administration that’s been almost completely deferential to the interests of businesses looking to get bigger. Since his return to office, the Department of Justice has filed exactly one lawsuit to block a merger—the $14 billion acquisition of Juniper Networks by the Hewlett Packard Enterprise Company—only to quickly reach a settlement. Stanley Woodward, a top DOJ official who oversees antitrust enforcement, appears to be an active opponent of antitrust litigation. Bloomberg reports that he’s wondered aloud in meetings why DOJ can’t simply stop reviewing deals. That’s not exactly the guy you want in charge of preventing monopolies. As the Live Nation case shows, even when a lawsuit does move forward, CEOs can simply go to Trump to get his support. After describing the settlement as “shocking” to lawmakers on Capitol Hill in May, Roger Alford, a Notre Dame law professor and former Antitrust Division senior official in Trump’s first term, observed that Trump’s interference in the case both undermined norms and set a new precedent for behind-the-scenes advocacy: “The rule of law quickly becomes the rule of lobbyists.” Read: Even when Ticketmaster works, it doesn’t Presidents historically avoided interfering directly with antitrust cases. The exceptions were few and notoriously corrupt (such as Richard Nixon’s meddling in an antitrust lawsuit against the International Telephone & Telegraph Corporation in exchange for a financial donation to the Republican National Convention). But Trump views the entire executive branch as an extension of his will, and he has turned regulatory agencies and DOJ into tools he can use to dole out favors, reward friends, and punish enemies. The only companies that have to worry about a federal antitrust prosecution at this point are those that Trump fiercely dislikes. But as the Live Nation case demonstrates, any sighs of relief among leaders of America’s oversize businesses would be premature. In an ideal world, antitrust regulations wouldn’t be fragmented and enforced by a panoply of states. But when the president effectively turns national antitrust laws into dead letters, the virtues of federalism become apparent: States will do what DOJ fails to do. This arrangement also makes it harder for companies to simply lobby their way to good deals across multiple states. The paradox is that by trying to hand out sweetheart arrangements to companies that are either trying to merge or flexing their monopoly power, Trump has made it more likely that those companies will have to navigate a gantlet of state lawsuits. And in asserting total control over the federal government, Trump has ended up making state governments much more aggressive and influential than they were before.

The Vicious Cycle Trump May Restart
2026-09-03

The Vicious Cycle Trump May Restart

The Atlantic | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
By the time he retired from the Marine Corps, in 1931, Major General Smedley Butler had served in Mexico, Haiti, Cuba, the Philippines, Nicaragua, the Dominican Republic, Honduras, and China. It was the golden age of American imperialism, and Butler was one of its golden boys, among the most decorated fighters in U.S. history. But over time, Butler began to have doubts about what he was doing. “I spent 33 years and 4 months in active service,” he wrote in 1935. “During that period I spent most of my time being a high-class muscle man for Big Business, for Wall Street and for the bankers. In short, I was a racketeer; a gangster for capitalism.” The costs of these excursions, he wrote, were paid in taxpayer money and soldiers’ lives. The wars that Butler fought also left scars around the world, as the journalist Jonathan M. Katz wrote in a 2022 biography. Little remembered in the United States, these interventions were formative for the populations they affected, fueling enduring anti-Americanism. The Trump administration seems determined to re-create the cycle in Venezuela today: military action, puppet government, exploitation of oil reserves, and almost certainly more resentment, fueling further conflict in the future. In January, U.S. troops descended on Venezuela and seized President Nicolás Maduro. Although President Trump offered federal drug-trafficking charges against Maduro as window dressing, he made no secret of the real reason for the incursion: He wanted to seize the country’s huge oil reserves. This week, the president claimed that he would refill the U.S. Strategic Petroleum Reserve with Venezuelan oil, “a Gift from Venezuela to the People of the United States.” The Trump administration has, as usual, offered few concrete details, but as my colleague Jonathan Chait writes, this is less a “deal” than “a payment by Venezuela of tribute at gunpoint.” The Venezuelan people are in no position to consent to handing over the country’s most valuable natural resource to a richer regional superpower for free, because Venezuela has not had fair elections in years. After deposing Maduro, the United States did not clear away his government or try to install the pro-democracy dissident (and Nobel Peace Prize laureate) María Corina Machado; in fact, it has created barriers to her return. Instead, it has enlisted a former Maduro lieutenant as president, while Secretary of State Marco Rubio oversees her as a sort of viceroy. Besides, nothing in international relations is as simple as a gift. The current arrangement involves Alejandro Betancourt López, a Venezuelan oil oligarch who has been under investigation in several countries. Betancourt managed to maintain connections with both Maduro’s regime and the U.S. government, and to dodge some charges thanks to help from Rudy Giuliani. Chevron is expanding its footprint in Venezuela, although other oil companies have been reluctant. Investors from other sectors, including crypto, are also getting involved. Although much money stands to be made, whether and how much ordinary Americans will actually benefit is less clear. Rebuilding Venezuelan production will take years, and the country’s oil is heavy and difficult to refine. That means no near-term relief at gas pumps and years before the U.S. reserve is refilled. Venezuelan oil and U.S. interests have a long and rocky history. American oil companies invested heavily in the country after World War II, but in 1976, the Venezuelan government nationalized the oil industry. In the early ’90s, the government once again opened to private companies. But Hugo Chávez, who portrayed himself as an anti-colonial hero and liked to use “Gringos go home!” as a slogan, became president in 1999. In 2007, he expropriated foreign assets once more, in what his energy minister called “an act of sovereignty for our country, for our people.” Trump cited the expropriation this year as another reason for U.S. intervention. Chávez’s approach, maintained under his protégé Maduro, failed. The oil industry, along with the rest of the national economy, has atrophied. In an article last year, my colleague Gisela Salim-Peyer eloquently recounted how Venezuela slid into authoritarianism under Chávez and Maduro. International observers described the 2018 presidential election as rigged, and evidence gathered by Machado convincingly showed that her party had won the 2025 race. A huge number of people fleeing Venezuela have flocked to the United States. For now, under the rump Chávez-Maduro regime, Venezuelans don’t have much say, but guessing how this might play out doesn’t require a great deal of creativity—just a working knowledge of history. Trump’s expropriation of Venezuelan oil won’t be well received by most people, nor will a Trump-driven gold rush by American businesses, no matter how much Venezuela needs assistance standing up its oil industry. Railing against the deal and foreign oil interests will be an easy populist stance for future Venezuelan politicians; perhaps one will even feel moved to nationalize assets once more—and so the cycle can begin again. You don’t have to be an anti-colonial peacenik to worry about the risks of Trump’s oil raid. You can just grasp that having hostile demagogues in power in the hemisphere is bad for U.S. interests, driving global instability as well as the kind of mass migration that Trump has described as a crisis. Such downsides help explain why anti-interventionism had become popular among Republicans over the past decade. Until the Venezuela raid, Trump portrayed himself as “America first,” but the foremost voice was J. D. Vance, who cited his experience deployed in Iraq in his speech accepting the GOP nomination for vice president. “As always, America’s ruling class wrote the checks. Communities like mine paid the price,” he said, sounding much like his fellow Marine Smedley Butler. “For decades, that divide between the few, with their power and comfort in Washington, and the rest of us only widened.” Yet now the Trump administration has embarked on just the sort of adventure Butler led and then decried. Wealthy Americans will benefit today, ordinary Americans will see little immediate reward, and the United States will face the blowback for decades to come. Here are three new stories from The Atlantic: Time-Travel Thursdays: Technology was once seen as countercultural, Jake Lundberg writes. He looks through The Atlantic’s archives to trace how that identity has evolved. The Netherlands Will Never Be Rid of Louisiana Crawfish Confronted with a seasoned, boiled, arguably delicious crayfish, some of the people of the Netherlands are not sure exactly what to do. “Do you suck the head out?” I heard from the table next to me earlier this summer, at Two Chefs, a restaurant in Amsterdam that hosts a monthly crayfish boil. Yes is the answer: The best way to eat a crayfish is to shell the tail, then slurp the flavored juices out of the head, and pick out the remaining meat with your hands. Reflect. The Met’s John Galliano exhibition had problems beyond the designer’s past transgressions, Kimberly Chrisman-Campbell argues. Explore. Faith Hill explores how “somatics” became the solution to modern life. The bluesman Son House’s classic “Death Letter” recounts the shock of hearing about a loved one’s death. I felt a little of that shock when I learned of the death of the singer Cassandra Wilson this week. She was just 70. As regular readers may know, I’m a jazz obsessive, but I don’t listen to many singers; jazz vocals too often come off as affected, cutesy, or outdated. Wilson wasn’t like that. She wasn’t like anybody, though she showed the influence of the great Abbey Lincoln. Wilson, a member of the influential collective M-Base, had real avant-garde credentials, but she also loved to adapt homelier material: Bob Dylan, Miles Davis, the Monkees, and old folk songs, all of it made personal and new. Her career was a forceful reminder that although the music we label “Americana” is heavily white and male, it couldn’t exist without Black women. To my ear, Wilson was also the greatest blues singer of our era. Her rendition of “Death Letter” is as haunting, visceral, and deep as any recording in the genre’s long history. Rafaela Jinich contributed to this newsletter. When you buy a book using a link in this newsletter, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic.

How the Early Dreams of the Internet Changed
2026-09-03

How the Early Dreams of the Internet Changed

The Atlantic | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
This is an edition of Time-Travel Thursdays, a journey through The Atlantic’s archives to contextualize the present. Sign up here. During the 1984 Super Bowl, an advertisement launched a technological revolution. If you were alive, you probably know the one. Directed by Ridley Scott, the minute-long spot showed men in gray uniforms, heads shaved, eyes blank, marching in unison toward a lifeless auditorium. A Big Brother figure speaks from a giant screen, droning on about “the first glorious anniversary of the Information Purification Directives.” All the while, a woman in red shorts and a white tank top is running to the auditorium. She looks like she’s just come from an aerobics class, but she has a sledgehammer, and authorities are in pursuit. Entering the room, she hurls the sledgehammer into Big Brother’s face. There’s a flash of light, and a wind blows over the stunned, awakened audience. A different voice—a comforting one—announces, “On January 24th, Apple Computer will introduce Macintosh. And you’ll see why 1984 won’t be like 1984.” Although the ad did not show an actual Macintosh computer, it worked. Apple went on to sell 250,000 of its user-friendly, mouse-driven little machines in that year alone. The ad also asserted what was to be a defining idea of the emerging digital age: that personal computers, far from massive instruments of centralized control, were vessels of individual freedom. The coming of the internet, with its invitation to universally accessible knowledge and the free exchange of ideas, seemed to affirm the promise: Our machines wouldn’t enslave us; they would liberate us. The dystopia depicted in the ad came by way of George Orwell’s imagination, but the fears underlying it had deep American roots. As mass consumer culture expanded in the early 20th century, American artists, intellectuals, and critics worried over the problem of “conformity” and the loss of individualism that came with it. Most didn’t see the dark totalitarianism of 1984; instead, they saw a world of ready-made desires, a soulless existence brought to you by corporations, advertisers, media, and politicians. As The Atlantic’s review of Sinclair Lewis’s Babbitt put it in 1922, “the ‘standardized American’” was becoming “as much a national product as the Ford car”—a person like Babbitt himself, whose entire life and very personality were shaped by forces beyond him. Such criticism formed a fault line that ran from the cultural commentary of the ’20s to the outbursts of Beats, folkies, and academics in the ’50s. The earthquake, of course, would come in the counterculture of the 1960s. No longer would people need to be stuffed into “Little Boxes,” as the folk singer Malvina Reynolds worried early in the decade. The counterculture promised liberation from such confinement. Visiting San Francisco’s Haight-Ashbury district for The Atlantic in 1967, the novelist Mark Harris wondered whether he was seeing Babbitt’s descendants finally rebelling against conformity in ways that Babbitt himself could not. The counterculture’s sensibilities migrated into the mainstream, where popular culture and advertising absorbed its language of liberation and transgression, so that admen could pitch 7 Up using hippie iconography or Virginia Slims cigarettes by way of the women’s-liberation movement. Digital culture would prove especially receptive to these countercultural ideals. Northern California, after all, was the center of both the counterculture and the tech industry, and the cross-pollination between the two was considerable, as the historian Fred Turner has shown. By the 1990s, as the internet was becoming a mass medium, some of its first boosters (many of whom had roots in the counterculture) were presenting it as the realization of countercultural ideals. John Perry Barlow, a poet and sometimes Grateful Dead lyricist, wrote his 1996 “Declaration of the Independence of Cyberspace” in response to an early effort to regulate the internet. It reads like a Port Huron Statement for digital culture, describing a realm of individual freedom and free-flowing ideas that would break from existing forms of cultural and institutional authority. According to Barlow, a new “civilization of the Mind” was coming. In the dot-com and digital revolutions that followed, many of the people who sold the hardware, coded the software, and built the platforms were seen as iconoclasts themselves. Digital entrepreneurship became a form of transgression, not just in the casual dress and informal culture of the Silicon Valley office but also in what it promised to users. Tech visionaries pledged to create a world that would make possible precisely the kind of self-expression, open exchange, and communal connection that the counterculture had promised. It was no coincidence that by the late 1990s, Apple’s advertising—again with almost no reference to the machines themselves—simply pictured groundbreaking figures such as Gandhi, Martin Luther King Jr., Bob Dylan, and John Lennon, alongside the slogan “Think Different.” In many ways, those promises were fulfilled. The internet did splinter 20th-century mass culture, and the effects have been every bit as revolutionary as promised. We don’t all watch the same networks anymore; we don’t see the same movies; we don’t get our news from the same places or read the same books (mostly, we just don’t read); we “do our own research.” Even as the conditions of mass culture and conformity that shaped the counterculture and the early dreams of the internet have faded, we continue to live in their shadow. In the fractured age of social media and the unknowable algorithm, our devices give us the feeling of freedom, even as they serve the interests of corporate forces that track our movements, catalog our interests, and profile our wants. The oppressive monoculture has been replaced by countless minicultures, each with their own devoted acolytes; we still inhabit little boxes, partially of our own making. The transgressive ethos, meanwhile, continues to thrive. Subversion is perhaps the dominant language of digital culture, spoken across the political spectrum by podcasters “just asking questions,” wellness gurus swilling at-home health hacks, manosphere kingpins lashing out against wokeness, and lefty influencers denouncing the billionaire class. Everyone, it seems, is hurling a sledgehammer at something.

The Hasan Piker Question
2026-09-03

The Hasan Piker Question

The Atlantic | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
Like late-summer humidity, Hasan Piker hangs heavy over the discourse. At a rally on Monday, Vice President Vance brought up the hard-left streamer in an attempt to paint the Democratic Senate candidate Abdul El-Sayed as anti-American. Last week, House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries called Piker’s recent statements about Jewish Americans  “dangerous and anti-Semitic.” Senators Jon Ossoff, Cory Booker, and Jacky Rosen said much the same. Facing questions about a totally different set of Piker comments, Representative Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez hustled into an elevator. Some Democrats are trying to dismiss the 35-year-old political commentator as irrelevant. He’s just “a streamer in California,” El-Sayed said on Saturday, waving off questions from reporters. Piker himself has spent the past week casting himself as the victim of hysteria. “I have become this media cycle’s ‘trans athletes’ or ‘migrant caravan,’” he posted on X. But it was El-Sayed who first made Piker central to his campaign by bringing him out on the trail, partying poolside with him at an influencer event, and celebrating with him during his Election Night rally. In his thousands of hours of streaming, Piker has produced a dense catalog of comments spanning the spectrum from stupid to incredibly offensive, including several iterations of the phrase “I hate America” and, perhaps most memorably, that “America deserved 9/11.” (He quickly attempted to walk it back.) Piker also flaunts his authoritarian sympathies, calling Mao Zedong “one of the great leaders of this world” and counting the collapse of the Soviet Union among the “catastrophes” of the 20th century. Last week, he warned that “eventually someone is going to come around and take action” against American Jews who are “singularly invested in Israel.” Hasan Piker is part of the conversation now. He has become central to the Republican strategy in Michigan, where El-Sayed will continue to be asked about him. So, too, will Democrats across the country. The question ahead of November is whether their answers will satisfy voters. El-Sayed has moved away from Piker since winning the primary last month. The streamer has not been on the trail in Michigan. “I have spoken with the good doctor,” Representative Jim Clyburn told reporters of El-Sayed, who has a medical degree. “He has apologized. He says he will not campaign with Piker anymore.” After Piker’s comments last week about American Jews, El-Sayed said in a statement that anti-Semitism is a “scourge” and that “I stand against any rhetoric that puts the community at risk.” When asked about Piker at a meeting with a group of Jewish Democrats on Saturday, though, El-Sayed declined to disavow him. “If you want to make this race about a streamer in California, I just think you’re missing the boat.” Anyone could have predicted the Piker blowback. Plenty of people did. In March, one of El-Sayed’s primary opponents, Mallory McMorrow, compared Piker to the white-supremacist influencer Nick Fuentes, and leaders of the centrist think tank Third Way wrote a column urging Democrats not to associate with Piker. “Everyone saw it coming from a mile away, so they should’ve been better prepared to handle it,” one Democratic strategist told me. Even some of El-Sayed’s own backers felt similarly. “Not all socialism is toxic to the Democratic brand, but Piker certainly is,” one progressive strategist told me. (None of the Democrat-aligned operatives I reached out to would agree to speak on the record for this story, citing their support for El-Sayed as well as their fear of reprisal from Piker’s rabid online fans.) The Piker drama is a ribbon-wrapped gift to Republicans in a year when so much else—inflation, high gas prices, an unpopular president—is working against them. They seem well aware of this. Mike Rogers, El-Sayed’s Republican opponent, refers to Piker as El-Sayed’s “running mate.” “When I see Abdul El-Sayed campaign around this state with somebody who said that America deserved 9/11, what I see is a man who has sympathy and compassion for people who share his religion, but has nothing for people who don’t,” Vance said at the rally on Monday. “What I’d ask you to remember is that whoever you send to Washington, whatever policy views you have or you don’t have, send people of good heart who focus on the right issues, who fight for the right people, and who love the United States of America.” The strategy is obvious: Identify every repellent thing Piker has ever said, tie Piker to El-Sayed, and advertise the relationship at every opportunity. There is more than a hint of irony in this situation. President Trump has shown a strong affinity for shock jocks and trolls. In 2020, he awarded Rush Limbaugh, who had a recurring segment celebrating the deaths of gay people from AIDS, the Presidential Medal of Freedom. Laura Loomer, a far-right activist and an ally of Trump’s, last week referred to a Senate candidate as a “ghetto black communist bitch.” Some Democrats, seeing Trump suffer little political consequence for such associations, believe it’s unfair that El-Sayed should have to answer for Piker’s actions. But politics, like life, is famously unfair. Democrats are constantly being asked to condemn Piker in cable-news hits and hallway interviews, even outside Michigan. Several Jewish Democrats have weighed in too. Through a spokesperson, Ossoff said he “unreservedly rejects reckless and dangerous rhetoric suggesting that policy advocacy invites violence against the Jewish community or any community.” Representative Josh Gottheimer of New Jersey said that “anyone who campaigns with Piker or elevates him is complicit in the rising antisemitism and anti-American hatred in this country.” Moderate Democrats are seizing the moment as a chance to engage in a little centrist posturing. Senator John Fetterman went on Fox News and delivered a challenge to El-Sayed: “I’m offering $50,000 for you to do a rally with Hasan Piker” on September 11, he said. Piker was not immediately available for comment for this story. But last week, after several Jewish Democrats criticized him, he dismissed their concerns by implying they reflected an orchestrated conspiracy against him, writing on X that an “aipac boss call was made.” Maybe El-Sayed could quell some of the frenzy himself. In 2008, controversial comments from Barack Obama’s former pastor Jeremiah Wright threatened to upend Obama’s bid for the White House. In a sermon shortly after September 11, the pastor said that “America’s chickens are coming home to roost.” Later, Wright said, “No, no, no, not God Bless America. God damn America—that’s in the Bible—for killing innocent people.” Obama chose to address the controversy directly, and delivered a speech condemning Wright’s remarks while offering context about America’s racial tensions. That speech “was a masterpiece of empathy, and at the time it felt that it changed the trajectory of his campaign,” Michelle Goldberg wrote this week. At the very least, some Democrats argue, El-Sayed could more plainly denounce his friend. “Say, ‘Here’s what I condemn; here’s what I disavow. I disagree; it was wrong and hurtful,’” the progressive strategist said. “If Piker says something new that’s bad—which, if I was a betting man, I’d say will happen—you come out against it swiftly.” Michigan, a state that Trump won in 2016 and 2024, was never going to be an easy win for El-Sayed. His relationship with Piker, who is paid to provoke, not persuade, isn’t going to make that task any simpler. The good news for Democrats is that most people don’t actually know Piker; polling from earlier this year showed that 60 percent of likely voters have never heard of him. The bad news for Democrats is that Republicans will spend the next two months working diligently to change that.

An Alien Scholar’s Notes on Trump’s Washington
2026-09-03

An Alien Scholar’s Notes on Trump’s Washington

The Atlantic | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
We’ll start this conference looking back on 2026 Washington Architecture by being honest: We have no idea what happened here. The snapshot of this planet that my comrades and I managed to obtain on our brief visit left us, if anything, more confused about Earth than we had been before. What follows are theories only. All we know of 21st-century human political figure Donald J. Trump is what we can guess from the architectural record and a few bits of digital evidence. (Due to a misunderstanding, we ate most of the written records of this planet before figuring out that they contained information.) Was he a builder or a destroyer? There are also ruins of an arch—commemorating a triumph, but over what? Perhaps the successful defeat of 250 years of democracy, a system of rule that he despised? Did he have access to the internet (a human technology that seems to have been primarily used for the sharing of pornography and misinformation)? Impossible to say. Some records suggest that a female of his species accessed the internet on his behalf. We don’t know his exact relation to this female, but we can observe that she was swift of foot and skilled at concealment in small enclosures, probably a trait admired by others. Were such relationships common? Did many human leaders consider it an honor to be chased by another human carrying a printer? (Did George Washington have a “Human Printer” of his own? Perhaps Benjamin Franklin fulfilled this role?) Although other human figures enjoyed construction, Trump seemed to prefer the wrecking ball (what we would refer to as Grogon’s Tail) as a mode of expression. From a forensic analysis of these structures, we know that the Smashing of the Residence was first to take place, followed by the Desecration of the Pool. Donald Trump seems to have smashed the Residence deliberately, as a warning to rivals. As his nestlings were at that time all mature and dwelling independently, such a gesture posed no danger to his young. Some papers we found suggested that there were plans to put something in the enormous hole, but these drawings are on an unlikely scale, and their suggested purpose is inconsistent. Ballroom? Bunker? No one can seem to agree, not even the plans themselves. The situation of the pool is puzzling. We may never know why Donald Trump hated the pool (pond?) so much, but his action to strip it of its water, paint over its interior with a humiliating sealant, and fill it with a thick green algae speaks of immense disrespect. Had the pool or pond offended him? We know from the many banners he left bearing his visage around the city that he seems to have been incapable of smiling; a golden idol located in Florida represents him as having a slightly melted appearance. Perhaps glimpsing his reflection in the pond reminded him unpleasantly of these facts? Or were its ducks hostile to him? Some records show that even when the pool was drained of water, it was patrolled by an armed guard; what danger lurked in its shallows? The structure with the giant head in it puzzles us most. We think this site on the Potomac may once have been used for entertainment and functioned as a kind of temple to the performing arts. Others suggest that it was an oversize dwelling place for mice. Some scholars believe that people really went inside the building and sat in red chairs while someone stood and bellowed in Italian. Perhaps this hall was used for punishment? Were wrongdoers subjected to the “tuba”? Letters found near the site marking it “The Donald J. Trump and the John F. Kennedy Memorial Center for the Performing Arts” suggest that the enormous bronze head may represent either Donald J. Trump or John F. Kennedy, in whose joint memory the center seems to have been erected. Who were these men? They must have loved the arts. Perhaps an American Harmodius and Aristogeiton? Curiously, we do not see them commemorated jointly in other locations. Some scholars argue that Donald J. Trump was in fact alive at the time of the center’s commemoration, but other scholars reply that this claim is ludicrous—where else is there evidence of a Memorial Center constructed while its subject was still living? The same theory suggests that he added his name to the center decades after its construction, halted all the shows, removed the public artworks, and threatened to demolish it if his name was not kept on, but other scholars reply that smashing things unless you can write your name on them is hardly the behavior of a full-fledged adult. We truly don’t know what to make of this place. (Just for the record, though, we didn’t do the pyramids.)

The Sexual Politics of the ‘Bed Trick’
2026-09-03

The Sexual Politics of the ‘Bed Trick’

The Atlantic | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
A decade ago in northern England, a young woman named Gayle Newland was convicted—twice—of an unusual crime. Another woman, who was known variously as “Chloe” and “Miss X” in court documents and media reports, reported that she’d been a victim of sexual fraud. Newland, she said, had posed as a chronically ill man named Kye Fortune in order to become her lover; the gender deception was enabled by the use of a blindfold during sex. But whenever Newland took the witness stand to testify, she shared an entirely different version of events. She and Miss X were best friends and closeted lesbians, she said, and Kye was their shared fantasy, sustained over several years. According to the British newspapers, particularly tabloids such as The Daily Mail and the Daily Express, the story was simple: Newland was a predator, carefully tricking her heterosexual friend into a lesbian relationship by pretending to be a man. The legal system and the juries affirmed this reporting, finding Newland guilty twice (the original conviction was overturned on a technicality). Newland’s actions resembled the kind of madcap fiction in which someone pretends to be the opposite sex and hijinks ensue (as in Twelfth Night or The Rocky Horror Picture Show); pretends to be someone’s husband in the dark (as in Chaucer’s The Canterbury Tales); or changes sex outright, raising deeper questions (as in Virginia Woolf’s Orlando). But Izabella Scott, whose new book, The Bed Trick, is an astoundingly well-calibrated mixture of legal reporting and literary analysis, knows full well that a heavily publicized trial is hardly the best venue for understanding other people—especially how other people have sex. “Trials are storytelling contests,” Scott writes. “They are making a case, producing stories—competitive stories that use extremes in order to win.” Scott is rightfully less interested in figuring out whether the verdict was correct (because it’s been litigated already) than in what the case illuminates about gender and truth. What preoccupies her is how the narrative device of the “bed trick,” so common in fiction and so rare in real life, came to fruition as a contest of wills between two young women. She wants to understand how two wildly disparate stories were created, what those stories tell us about ourselves, and what continues to make many people so anxious about relationships and behaviors that defy traditional norms. The Newland trials are rich texts, and Scott pores over every transcript page, looking for meaning. The case, one of sexual assault with elements of fraud, was developed in line with the U.K.’s most recent Sexual Offences Act, which was passed in 2003 and nullified consent in situations where “the defendant intentionally deceived the complainant as to the nature or purpose of the relevant act.” Scott defines this version of rape as “a violation of sexual autonomy—that is, a violation of personal freedom, of choice, and thus a violation of selfhood, identity, perhaps even humanity.” Read: An extraordinary account of a dangerous marriage Rape cases are notoriously difficult to prosecute; only a sliver of those reported (themselves a likely undercount of incidents) go to trial, and few result in a conviction. The Newland case was even more unusual, because the first conviction hinged on Section 76 of the Sexual Offences Act—the clause about deceit. (The second conviction drew on a different case, R v. McNally, which ruled that “deception as to gender can vitiate consent.”) What Miss X alleged was more complex than a simple violation of “no means no”: Because Miss X had been deceived, her yeses were built on a lie. This deception was multilayered, as Scott discovers and analyzes. Miss X came from a religious background, was estranged from her parents, and had reportedly ended an abusive relationship recently, making her especially vulnerable—whether as a closeted queer woman, as Newland attested, or as, according to Miss X, a straight woman in love with a chronically ill man whose face and body she never saw during sex. Newland first concocted the “Kye Fortune” character online at the age of 13, swiping pictures and videos from a half-Asian man named Carlo over several years. Other women reported having been duped by “Kye” online before discovering that their correspondent wasn’t a man. And after the 2017 conviction, jurors learned that Newland had also been convicted of financial fraud. But Newland stuck to her story, claiming that she and Miss X met at a gay bar, came out to each other, and shared in the “Kye” fantasy together, although they still felt compelled to stay in the closet. (The evidence for this narrative is not strong.) Newland grew up with loving parents, owned a car, and had an allowance, but she claimed that at the all-girls private school she attended, the word lesbian was a slur, conditioning her to feel lasting shame about her sexuality. She sobbed repeatedly while on the stand, even as her story wavered in its details. Scott is merciful toward Newland, affording her the benefit of the doubt that the media, and especially the legal system, couldn’t or wouldn’t. The legal acceptance of queer relationships is still a recent development, and delineating the ways in which queer sex can work—with objects and implements, with kink and other proclivities not traditionally associated with heterosexual relationships—seemed to baffle the barristers during direct questioning and cross-examination. For Scott, reading through the transcripts became an exercise in the absurd, more like Nikolai Gogol’s “The Nose” than a typical true-crime tale. How could it not, with almost every controversial issue—race, class, queerness, gender, disability rights, consent—under the bright lights? “To read their stories is to find both” Newland and Miss X “entering a topsy-turvy world,” Scott writes, “their reason clouded by fantasy and hope, blinkered and madly doting. Both stepped over some line and their reason evaporated.” Read: Matt Lauer’s accuser complicates her story The loss of reason wasn’t exclusive to these young women, once so intimate and now such bitter adversaries. The Newland trials foreshadowed later debates about gender fluidity and sexuality, culminating in the 2025 U.K. Supreme Court ruling that the legal term woman refers to a biological woman. The Newland case featured an extreme manifestation of Britain’s cultural discomfort with shifting gender norms, but in the years since, that unease has led to decisions that are damaging the lives of many people who simply want to be left alone to be themselves. In spite of Scott’s rigorous forensic examination of the case, it is impossible to know with certainty what happened between Newland and Miss X. Their stories diverged markedly, but that divergence remained largely consistent across the trials: two irreconcilable versions of one relationship. Scott suggests that Newland and Miss X found the plots they needed to make themselves whole, as individuals and as a couple—whether they were best friends or closeted lesbian lovers. Scott knows that she, too, is telling a story. “In my attempt to know, I construct a plot,” she writes. “In a way, this book is a shadow trial.” She writes that she is “using literary tools, like suspense and points of view,” and implores readers never to forget this. In the process, she puts storytelling itself on trial: “I think about the stories that grip us in life, but also in court. I wonder why some stories are more believable than others.” Wisely, she doesn’t offer a grand unified theory of what happened between Miss X and Newland. She cannot. She knows the limits of narrative too well to do so. Still, the crime reporter in me latched on to a particular narrative contradiction, one that might point to the inciting incident that pulled the thread of deception and unraveled what was supposed to be the shared story. Newland and Miss X agree that it all fell apart one night. In Miss X’s version, “something just felt different.” Newland had insisted on oral sex; Miss X was reluctant, and the act was rough. Shortly afterward, she testified, she took her blindfold off and realized “Kye” was a woman—and had been using a prosthesis. In Newland’s version, their fight that evening resulted from Newland’s decision to come out as gay—a violation of their “pact.” Although Newland denied having oral sex, I wondered why Scott doesn’t spend more time exploring a third narrative: that this was a moment of muddled consent in a complicated situation. It seems plausible to me that Newland went ahead with a sexual act her partner simply didn’t want. And in the aftermath, she might have realized that the game had gone too far, and that the trust she’d been so careful to build was broken forever. Read: Everything we know about rape is wrong When she began working on The Bed Trick, Scott thought it would be primarily an exploration of rape law, but by the end, she realized it had more to do with the relationship between the law and fiction. In the process, she also dug up her own buried memories of a long-ago assault in a different country, an incident that proved to be so emotionally painful, and the bureaucracy involved in pursuing the case so burdensome, that she ended up dropping it. But the connections between her experience and the Newland case aren’t simple, just as the trial she writes about occupies all manner of messy borders. “There is no final answer,” she writes, “to what is deception and what is true.” Newland ultimately served a total of six years in prison. In 2024, a fashion influencer took to TikTok to recount her own astounding story: Her girlfriend, whom she’d featured in at least one video, had been revealed by various commenters to be Newland. The influencer ended the relationship. Newland, it seems, won’t be allowed to move on from her past. Miss X, at least, has the cloak of privacy to protect her. ​When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic.

The Wild Way Out of the Iran War That Still Manages to Be Better Than Any Alternative
2026-09-04

The Wild Way Out of the Iran War That Still Manages to Be Better Than Any Alternative

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. President Donald Trump is reportedly having private discussions with senior aides about whether he should end his war with Iran. The story, in the Wall Street Journal, rouses memories of Vermont Sen. George Aiken’s suggestion, early in the Vietnam War, that we should just “declare victory and go home.” Aiken didn’t really say quite that, but it would have been good if President Lyndon Johnson had taken up the idea then. And it would be very good if Trump followed his instincts—at least this instinct—now. He could say, as he has said many times, that he’s “obliterated” Iran’s nuclear program, that spaceborne cameras are constantly watching the area where its enriched uranium is buried deep inside mountains, and that if the Iranians try to extract it, we’ll bomb the site again. He could also recite statistics on the percentage of Iran’s missiles, ships, airplanes, etc., we’ve destroyed in the six-month war. These pronouncements would be either misleading or irrelevant. For example, the claim about Iran’s nuclear facilities has been true since his “Midnight Hammer” bombing raid in June 2025, well before the start of this war. The claim about decimating Iran’s military forces neglects the fact that they’re still able to stop traffic in the Strait of Hormuz and inflict damage on U.S. bases throughout the Middle East, which is why the war carries on. But Trump’s supporters—the only audience he cares about pleasing—will overlook these awkward facts and cheer his declaration of “Mission accomplished.” The rest of us will view these claims as another set of lies—but we will also take some comfort that at least the war is over. The fact is, the more Trump prolongs the war, the weaker the United States seems. It is pathetic enough that our air and naval forces—by most measures the mightiest in the world—have hit more than 15,000 targets in Iran, yet failed to accomplish any strategic goal. In fact, it did worse than nothing. The barrage, which among other things killed the top echelon of Iran’s political and spiritual leaders, only elevated the senior officers of Iran’s elite military unit, which is much more hard-line and oppressive—and is now in complete control of the country. Another unforeseen effect of the barrage was to show that America’s military wasn’t as strong as it seemed. In the course of hitting those 15,000 targets, the U.S. Air Force and Navy depleted a stunningly high percentage of the country’s most vital munitions—including more than half of its cruise missiles and high-tech air-defense weapons. The campaign also required replacing the USS Abraham Lincoln, one of the two aircraft carriers mobilized for the war, with the USS George Washington, which had been the only aircraft carrier the Navy had in the Pacific Fleet. (The Lincoln had to rotate out because early in the war, Iranian missiles ravaged the Navy’s supply hub in Bahrain, leaving the crew of 5,000 sailors bereft of fresh food and basic supplies.) The whole world is watching. What they’re seeing is pretty grim—a onetime superpower that no longer evokes much respect or fear. There’s no sign that appearances will soon brighten. In Iran, U.S. military pressure isn’t working, while economic pressure has its limits, as long as the Chinese keep helping Iran (as they’re likely to do, it being in their interest to keep the United States bottled up in the Middle East, far away from the Taiwan Strait and the South China Sea). Trump doesn’t know what to do. Nor does anyone around him. They are so desperate for ideas that a month ago, a senior intelligence officer in U.S. Central Command, which plans and executes military operations in the Middle East, emailed a broad group of analysts and officers, asking for “new creative and unconventional ways to pressure and punish Iran.” They—and thus we—are in a trap. The more Trump threatens to destroy all of Iran if they don’t do what he says (as he did again for the umpteenth time this week), and then backs off (which is better than following through but doesn’t do much for our image), the less credible any of our threats or promises seem. It is no surprise, then, that allies are looking elsewhere for supply chains and security—in part out of fear that our common adversaries may be tempted to take aggressive action in this vulnerable moment for America. We would all be better off if Trump just ended the war. Stop attacking Iran (and thus provoking Iran to attack us). Let Iran and Oman work out an arrangement to open the Strait of Hormuz; it would probably entail tolls, but that’s better than the uncertainties and grave costs of the current situation. Meanwhile, keep an eye on the potential revival of Iran’s nuclear program—which, for the moment, even the CIA concludes, poses no imminent threat. It would be better still if Trump then sent the USS George Washington back to the Pacific, fired Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth for giving him lousy advice (it wouldn’t be the first time Trump turned a loyal aide into a scapegoat), and made polite phone calls to all of our allies (whom he’s lambasted for not helping him fight Iran), assuring them that he would consult with them before starting any wars in the future. This, of course, is fantasy (except maybe the part about sending the Washington back to its port in Japan). It’s certainly what any normal president would do—more to the point, it’s what any normal president would have done all along. That only amplifies how far Donald Trump is from normal. News and Politics - Slate Magazine
Trump’s Latest Attack on American Education Is Particularly Nefarious
2026-09-03

Trump’s Latest Attack on American Education Is Particularly Nefarious

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Since Donald Trump returned to office, his administration has waged a war on supposed “wokeness” in education. On Thursday, it proposed new federal regulations that would target schools that explicitly aim to help nonwhite students. Here we go again. What exactly does this new rule say? Schools already have to meet certain requirements to qualify for 501(c)(3) tax-exempt status with the IRS. The new rule, which has yet to be finalized but is set to take effect in May, would add another. It states that any private school, trade school, or university that uses “race, color, or national or ethnic origin” in “admissions, educational policies, scholarships and loans, athletics, and every other school-administered or school-supported program” would no longer qualify for tax-exempt status. Many institutions have adopted policies to aid minority and underprivileged students in an attempt to balance out legacies of discrimination in both higher education and American society. But the Trump administration has cast those efforts as discriminatory against the students’ white and Asian peers. OK, but what would this rule mean in practical terms? Enforcing these guidelines would probably require IRS audits to determine whether a school’s policies or programs are in compliance. But the rule would seem to be worded broadly enough that a school that in any way supports, say, a club for Black students could conceivably be at risk. The IRS estimates that the new rule could strip scholarships from about 750,000 students and threaten the tax-exempt status of up to 18,000 schools nationwide, from private elementary schools to colleges. I get why that would be bad for students. But why is losing tax exemption bad for schools? Tax exemption, which private schools receive because education has long been considered a public good, saves some schools millions of dollars a year. It also lets people who give money to them deduct the donations from their taxes, an incentive that helps schools fundraise. Losing it could exacerbate financial struggles that have already caused some colleges to cut programs, lay off staff, and even close. Hang on: I thought the Supreme Court already outlawed affirmative action. So why is this even necessary? The new rule is part of an ongoing struggle to interpret the court’s landmark 2023 ruling, which banned race-conscious college admissions policies. In response, some schools have cut back on programs aimed at helping minority students, even relatively benign initiatives like special orientation events, and Black and Latino enrollment at top universities has fallen. But other schools have looked for wiggle room, and the new IRS rule does have some exceptions. It lets institutions continue to support underprivileged students as long as those efforts use “race-neutral criteria such as family income, geographic location, first-generation status, individual hardship, military family status, or academic achievement.” Even so, as schools try to walk Trump’s line under the new rule, they risk huge consequences if they err in a way the administration considers excessively “woke.” According to the New York Times, the administration already expects a suit from the American Association of University Professors, a group that supports academic freedom and has successfully challenged other Trump efforts to target higher education. And if the IRS does start auditing individual schools once the rule takes effect, the schools could contest attempts to take away their tax-exempt status in court. Threatening an organization’s tax-exempt status seems like an effort to intimidate it into complying in advance. Yes. Even the prospect of being investigated can lead organizations to steer clear of anything that might put them in Trump’s sights. In fact, that’s already happening. In previous cases, some colleges have settled with the administration (or are considering doing so) to end federal probes, or they’ve preemptively changed their policies to avoid scrutiny. Sometimes, controversy can be a tax in its own right. Now let’s pivot from higher education to higher purpose—like how to have a great rest of your evening. My colleagues think the following might help: A tribute to a feminist icon: Slate executive editor Susan Matthews (who also hosted Slow Burn: Roe v. Wade) remembers Gloria Steinem, who died on Wednesday at 92. Susan shares what she learned from Steinem’s long and successful career fighting for women’s rights. A movie so bad it’s good: Niu Lai, a poorly made Chinese film about animated cows, has quickly become one of the most profitable movies of all time. This feat is all the more impressive when you consider that “watching it is like mainlining a Pixar movie while severely concussed,” writes Franklin Schneider, who investigates why this amateur flick has been such a hit with audiences. Some dating advice for the modern era: Money is often a touchy subject in relationships, but especially when one person earns more than their partner. In the latest installment of Unhinged, Slate’s dating advice column, Steffi Cao counsels a woman whose dates are uncomfortable with her having the financial upper hand. Some wordplay to end the day: Take a break with today’s Pears, which has 10 rounds. Can you find the longest word? OK, it’s high time for me to leave you be for the night. See you tomorrow! Mike Johnson Just Made the Case for Expanding the Supreme Court
2026-09-03

Mike Johnson Just Made the Case for Expanding the Supreme Court

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Speaker of the House Mike Johnson led an effort Wednesday to amend the Constitution and cap the number of Supreme Court justices at nine. Since the size of the court is currently set by federal law, not by the Constitution, Johnson said the amendment was necessary to stop Democrats from adding justices to the court. Over the course of 24 hours, Johnson announced his plan to introduce the amendment and had it brought to the House floor, only for it to immediately fail along party lines. Despite this defeat, the debate over the court’s structure is just getting started. And Johnson’s “Stop the Count” strategy proves just how much reform is needed. It doesn’t take a brain surgeon (or even a lawyer) to understand why the leader of the House GOP would like to keep in place a Supreme Court with six Republican appointees. But these kinds of legislative efforts are extremely rare coming from any speaker. Although it was Arizona Rep. Andy Biggs who formally introduced the resolution, Wednesday marked the first time in 30 years that a House speaker has led the charge on proposing a constitutional amendment. What’s more interesting, then, is not the proposal itself, which consisted of only a sentence, but the reason Johnson felt the need to introduce the measure in the first place. His main rationale is that the Constitution allows Congress to set the size of the court through legislation. Early on in American history, there were often only six justices, a number that Congress expanded periodically. The court’s size rose to 10 by around the Civil War and the creation of the 10th U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals, which covers the Mountain West states. The Judiciary Act of 1869 set the current size at nine. In short, the only thing stopping court expansion is a Democratic congressional majority willing to pass a single court-reform law and a Democratic president willing to sign it. And as New York Times columnist Jamelle Bouie put it recently, “Democrats are getting serious about court reform.” Former Vice President and Democratic nominee Kamala Harris has officially advocated for expanding the court from nine to 13 justices. A lawyer herself, Harris notes that because the country already has 13 intermediate courts of appeal, a 13-member Supreme Court would allow each justice to review motions from a lower court one-to-one. Former Secretary of Transportation and 2020 Democratic presidential candidate Pete Buttigieg has made the same 13-member argument in his long-running calls to expand the court, while also developing new rules to make the nomination process less partisan. And in an interview this week on Meet the Press, influential South Carolina Rep. Jim Clyburn noted support for court expansion. So a position that was once shared by only a handful of congressional politicians is now the dominant Democratic view. Helping the party’s cause is the Constitution, which already gives Congress immense control over the size and structure of the court. Article 3 declares that there must be a Supreme Court, but that it has authority to decide only a narrow category of cases, such as lawsuits between different states. All other types of cases are in the court’s purview merely because Congress decided to grant jurisdiction over that subject by passing a law. The people’s representatives also have the authority to make as many lower federal courts as they choose; Congress has steadily expanded that number as the country has increased in population, to the point that we now have 94 such courts. Finally, the Senate too plays a major role in determining which nominees get confirmed and removed. In some ways, because of the power of Congress over the courts, there are many reasonable and accessible avenues toward improving our judiciary. But Republican legislators would rather sabotage their own chamber’s constitutional authority than allow Democrats the chance to reform the court. In opposition to Johnson’s proposal, House Minority Whip Katherine Clark noted that the amendment would strip Congress of “yet another constitutional authority that is meant to be controlled by the American people’s representatives.” Think for a second about the moral calculus the GOP is doing. We’re talking about a system of congressional authority over the court that has lasted for 250 years. And Republicans are so scared of the electorate voting for judicial reform that they would eliminate a key piece of that century-old authority in the two months before they’re likely voted out of office. Speaking of failing to convince voters, Johnson’s call for constitutional self-castration is shared by his colleagues outside the House—especially those with a history of not getting the votes they want. Texas Sen. Ted Cruz introduced a similarly failed constitutional amendment for the upper congressional chamber last year. The politician, who ran for and lost the Republican nomination in 2016 and candidly stated last week that it’s “no secret that I want to be president,” is a decent standard-bearer for the views of his party as a whole. But before Cruz has another chance at the White House, the party remains led by Donald Trump, the man who sparked an insurrection on the nation’s Capitol after losing the 2020 election. A violent assault on the very same Congress that would vote Wednesday to cement this Trump-friendly court. During that election, Trump notoriously called for election officials to “stop the count!”—but only in the states that temporarily favored him while thousands of ballots were still being counted. Johnson’s approach to freezing the Supreme Court’s current size, right as its Republican nominees reshape American law, has the same antidemocratic aim. The fact remains that the court-reform debate is raging because we have a high court that is deeply detached from the public. A court committed to implementing the historical will of the long-dead people who first passed a law, while shrugging off the consequences its rulings have on people alive today. A court that avoids contending with ethics complaints against its own members, while blessing ethical violations in the White House. A court that demands the privacy of no cameras in its chambers, while allowing states to invade the private medical decisions of women across the country. A court that anonymously votes behind closed doors in secret rulings, and does so in support of Trump’s not-so-secret effort to disenfranchise votes by mail. Until all of that changes, court reform will be top of mind and top of discussion, no matter what the Republicans try to do before November. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism.
